Title: Quantum Machine Learning for Signature Forgery Detection

  • Authors: Nabila, Md Farhan Shahriyar, Nowshin Tabassum Puspa
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.ET
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33163
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33163
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33163]] Quantum Machine Learning for Signature Forgery Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33163)
  • Keywords: biometric
  • Abstract: Offline handwritten signatures remain a primary behavioural biometric in legal and financial documents. Detecting skilled forgeries from a static raster image is hard because pen pressure, timing and stroke order are missing. This work studies a family of quantum machine learning pipelines for offline signature forgery detection on a public Kaggle corpus of images split into two classes. We pair a ZZ feature map quantum kernel with three classical learners: K-Nearest Neighbours, Support Vector Machine and Random Forest. We then propose a deeper hybrid that uses EfficientNetB0 as a frozen feature extractor, reduces the resulting 1280 dimensional representation to a qubit register sized subspace through a quantum compatible principal component projection and decides class membership with a nearest neighbour rule. On a single held out test fold the proposed hybrid reaches 92.62 percent accuracy. On stratified five fold cross validation the same pipeline yields a mean accuracy of 91.26 percent with a standard deviation of 1.13 percent. The pipeline keeps a quantum embedding step that is portable to NISQ hardware. We report a controlled ablation across six quantum variants, confusion matrices, a t SNE projection and per class precision, recall and F1 score.

Title: Code availability is not enough: schemas for documenting and extending data analysis

  • Authors: H. Sherry Zhang, Roger D. Peng
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ME
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33167
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33167
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33167]] Code availability is not enough: schemas for documenting and extending data analysis(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33167)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: A data analysis involves a sequence of decisions, from data cleaning to model specification, each of which can affect the reported result. Reproducing that result is not the same as understanding the sequence of decisions that produced it and the reasons for each: the code mixes those decisions with implementation details, while the manuscript states the major decisions and their rationale but scatters them across its sections and often leaves out the minor ones. A reader may then ask how the result would change under a different decision: the question itself is easy to pose, but implementing it is often non-trivial in practice. In this work, we propose a schema that represents a data analysis as a collection of decisions in natural language, from which large language models (LLMs) can generate and validate analysis scripts directly. This workflow can also expand a single analysis into a full multiverse of alternatives, specified in an alternative file, ready for comparison. We demonstrate the proposed workflow in two applications. In the first, we construct a schema for a published regression analysis of heaped smoking data and show that it reproduces the reported estimates, and that a reviewer-suggested alternative can be specified through the alternative file and turned into code automatically. In the second, where no schema is provided, we show how the workflow serves instead as a reproducibility check, applied to the many-analysts football study. As an intermediate representation between the manuscript and code scripts, our schema gives a self-contained account of an analysis and makes it extensible from a reader's perspective.

Title: Temporal Exposure to Byzantine Vulnerability Under Intermittent Participation in Federated Learning

  • Authors: Sasa Maric Robert Abbas
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33168
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33168
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33168]] Temporal Exposure to Byzantine Vulnerability Under Intermittent Participation in Federated Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33168)
  • Keywords: robust, federate
  • Abstract: In this paper, we study the effect of participation variability on per-round Byzantine vulnerability. We formalise Dynamic Participation Exposure (DPE), a phenomenon in which the global Byzantine fraction remains below an aggregation rule's tolerance threshold, while a participating client set violates, or approaches violation of, the assumptions required for robust aggregation. Under stochastic participation, we derive the probability of per round exposure and analyse how active-set size affects the variability of the observed Byzantine fraction. We further examine the accumulation of exposure over multiple rounds and consider adversaries that selectively participate during favourable rounds. Our analysis shows that intermittent participation does not automatically imply aggregation failure. Rather, it transforms Byzantine robustness from a static population-level property into a stochastic and temporal property. To mitigate this risk, we outline actionable design recommendations based on minimum active-set constraints, temporal monitoring, participation history, and adaptive aggregation.

Title: MGMem: An Efficient, Deterministic, and Provenance-Preserving Framework for Long-Horizon Agent Memory

  • Authors: Junhong Huang, Xin Tong, Jun Xia
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33174
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33174
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33174]] MGMem: An Efficient, Deterministic, and Provenance-Preserving Framework for Long-Horizon Agent Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33174)
  • Keywords: generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Long-horizon Large Language Model (LLM) agents need persistent memory that surfaces the right past interaction at the right time across thousands of turns. Many systems index by invoking generative LLMs to rewrite history into summaries, facts, or knowledge graphs--a step that is (a) costly (thousands of generative calls per build), (b) non-deterministic (undermining reproducibility and giving inconsistent answers across runs), and (c) provenance-erasing (the reader sees synthesized text, not the original turn). We propose MGMem, a framework with no generative indexing calls that replaces this step with a deterministic discriminative-parser pipeline: recurring mentions across sessions are organized into an incidence graph, question mentions are resolved by a subject-conditioned three-stage resolver, and raw dialogue episodes are returned to the reader. On English multi-session dialogue QA, MGMem outperforms matched-protocol baselines on LoCoMo without generative indexing calls; on the full LongMemEval-S, it remains competitive overall and leads or ties on single-session types among local retrieval comparisons. Title: Feature-Based Explainability of Transformer Scoring Models: A Fidelity-First Framework for Automated Writing Assessment

  • Authors: Michael Hemenway, Edward W. Wolfe
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33175
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33175
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33175]] Feature-Based Explainability of Transformer Scoring Models: A Fidelity-First Framework for Automated Writing Assessment(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33175)
  • Keywords: explainability, transformer
  • Abstract: Transformer-based automated writing scoring models can achieve strong agreement with human scores while providing limited transparency into the response characteristics associated with their predictions. This study evaluated a feature-based post-hoc explanation approach in which explanatory fidelity was established before feature importance was interpreted. English Language Arts responses from three writing prompts in Grades 6 and 8 were analyzed, with about 12,700 responses per prompt and separate DeBERTa-v3-base research models for Expression and Conventions. Each response was represented by 61 interpretable linguistic and writing-quality features. Four approaches - theory-driven composites, hierarchical feature groupings, exploratory factor analysis, and Random Forest surrogates - were compared in their ability to reproduce DeBERTa predictions. Random Forest surrogates produced the largest observed R2 values across all six prompt-by-trait conditions, with held-out R2 values from .81 to .95. SHAP analyses showed substantial overlap in high-importance features across traits, with 75% to 90% of the top 20 features shared. Trait differentiation occurred mainly through relative feature weighting: Expression showed greater importance for lexical and conceptual indicators, whereas Conventions showed greater importance for sentence-level and syntactic-control indicators. Under researcher-defined mappings, rubric correspondence was 90%-95% for Expression and 12%-18% for Conventions. These percentages are descriptive and conditional on the mappings and available linguistic features. The findings support a fidelity-first framework for examining apparent scoring priorities while distinguishing post-hoc evidence about model behavior from direct explanation of transformer internals.

Title: Psychophysics of Duration Perception in Multimodal Large Language Models

  • Authors: Auss Abbood, Zihao Fu, Nigel Collier
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.SD, eess.AS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33176
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33176
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33176]] Psychophysics of Duration Perception in Multimodal Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33176)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Studies of time perception in language models focus mainly on text-based settings. Yet multimodal models are increasingly used for tasks that require perceiving the passage of time, making the limits of their temporal perception important to understand. We measure the limits of duration perception from audio against a human baseline to address this gap. Drawing from psychophysics, we operationalise duration perception as a melody recognition task, testing when a melody becomes unrecognisable as it is slowed down. Of 21 tested models, most cannot recognise well-known melodies without labelled in-context examples. With in-context examples, seven recognise melodies reliably, most peaking within the median human recognition band. Generalisation is limited: (i) recognition peaks shift with in-context example speed, (ii) melody titles induce prediction bias, and (iii) providing examples with scientific pitch notation does not prevent accuracy declining at long note durations for most models. In humans, the recognition window is a property of the perceiver; in current models, it is largely a property of the prompt.

Title: Geophysics-informed invertible operator network for Bayesian geophysical inversion

  • Authors: Yangwen Zhang, Xintong Wang, Xiaofei Guan, Peng Yu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33178
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33178
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33178]] Geophysics-informed invertible operator network for Bayesian geophysical inversion(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33178)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Geophysical inverse problems are inherently ill-posed because of sparse observations and measurement noise. Classical sampling-based Bayesian inference methods, such as Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), are computationally prohibitive because they require many forward simulations. Data-driven machine-learning methods often struggle to produce physically consistent solutions when training data are limited. To address these issues, we propose a geophysics-informed invertible operator network (GI-ION) that integrates an invertible neural network (INN) within a deep operator-learning framework for efficient Bayesian geophysical inversion. The INN serves as the branch network and transforms the model parameters into branch coefficients that represent the forward solution and a latent variable that captures uncertainty in the inverse problem. By learning the forward operator and enforcing statistical independence between the branch coefficients and the latent variable, GI-ION enables efficient sampling from a learned conditional approximation, even with sparse boundary observations. We validated GI-ION on three representative cases: 1D surface-wave dispersion and travel-time tomography for a 2D synthetic random-field model and a seismic-slice model. Numerical experiments demonstrate robust performance across diverse problem settings and efficient conditional sampling.

Title: Verified experience improves materials prediction with large language models

  • Authors: Yiming Zhang, Koji Tsuda
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33195
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33195
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33195]] Verified experience improves materials prediction with large language models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33195)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Materials discovery is an iterative loop: experiments and calculations verify predictions, and each outcome can inform the next. Yet most predictors are specialized single-task models fixed after training. Large language models have recently emerged as predictors across heterogeneous tasks but generally underperform specialized models, and retrieval-augmented variants discard their own outcomes. Benchmarks also score queries in isolation, leaving improvement from outcomes untested. Here we construct MatMemBench, a sequential benchmark of 22 materials tasks that reveals outcomes only after prediction and tests online adaptation, memory retention and cross-task transfer. We present MatRecall, a framework that lets large language models store verified predictions, with evidence, outcome and error, as episodes and recall them without retraining. MatRecall improved median performance by 62% over a memory-free model, exceeded specialized models on 11 tasks, remained useful after feedback stopped and transferred between related tasks. Verified experience could become a reusable resource for self-improving discovery loops.

Title: Extraneous Cognitive Load in Large Language Models

  • Authors: Arushi Gupta, Sheng Liu, James Zou
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33197
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33197
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33197]] Extraneous Cognitive Load in Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33197)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Cognitive Load Theory (CLT) shows that human problem-solving is affected by both intrinsic load, arising from task difficulty, and extraneous load, arising from how task information is presented. Large language models (LLMs) show analogous effects: presentation alone can affect model performance, but these effects have not yet been systematically isolated from task difficulty. To address this gap, we introduce CogLoadBench, a controlled benchmark that varies five CLT-inspired presentation factors across three reasoning task families. Across models, we find that higher extraneous load conditions often reduce accuracy by either adding competition or complicating the reasoning path. To support diagnosis and mitigation of load-related effects, we propose the Extraneous Load Score (ELS), a prompt-time metric computed from activations that estimates a model's extraneous load, and show that it generalizes across reasoning task types and load levels. We further show that shifting model representations toward lower-load directions at inference time can improve average model performance without changing prompt content.

Title: Confidence-Based Adaptive Retrieval Fails Where It Matters Most: A Paradox in Biomedical Question Answering

  • Authors: Chengying She, Siyu Deng, Haiyan Ge, Lizhuang Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33210
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33210
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33210]] Confidence-Based Adaptive Retrieval Fails Where It Matters Most: A Paradox in Biomedical Question Answering(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33210)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for biomedical question answering, but plausible incorrect answers remain a safety risk in clinical settings. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can ground responses in medical literature, while confidence-based adaptive RAG retrieves only when the model appears uncertain. We evaluate this routing strategy on three biomedical QA benchmarks of increasing difficulty and compare models across a broad range of parameter scales. The results reveal a paradox: confidence is well calibrated (AUROC 0.78--0.89) on standard medical exam questions where retrieval adds little value (+2.4\% to -1.4\%), yet becomes \emph{near-random} (AUROC 0.506) on specialist-level questions where retrieval helps most (+11.2\%). Five uncertainty signals---first-token probability, margin, entropy, consistency-based sampling, and verbalized confidence---show the same failure pattern. Five post-hoc calibration correction strategies, including density-ratio reweighting and rank-preserving transformation, do not recover a useful routing signal on hard questions (best AUROC: 0.507). Cross-model comparison shows that larger parameter scale and higher base accuracy do not guarantee reliable confidence-based routing under specialist-domain difficulty shift. These findings challenge the premise of confidence-based adaptive RAG: in high-stakes biomedical settings, defaulting to always-retrieve is safer and more accurate than confidence-based routing when calibration on the target domain is unknown.

Title: Demystifying Real-world LLM Service Freeloading

  • Authors: Xinyi Hou, Shenao Wang, Wenhui Pan, Yanjie Zhao, Haoyu Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33218
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33218
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33218]] Demystifying Real-world LLM Service Freeloading(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33218)
  • Keywords: defense
  • Abstract: Third-party projects repackage free inference from LLM web applications and dedicated clients into independent APIs. We refer to such use beyond providers' permitted access, allocation, or redistribution conditions as LLM service freeloading (LSF). However, its scale and underlying mechanisms remain poorly understood. We present a systematic measurement study of 513 open-source projects, tracing 1,588 inference paths across 64 upstream LLM services and characterizing their development, dependencies, and operating mechanisms. We find that 93.0% of repositories were created in 2026. Direct HTTP or WebSocket requests account for 83.2% of paths, making reproduction of product protocols the dominant invocation method. Projects adapt messages, reconstruct conversation state, and convert responses to support compatible APIs. Account selection or rotation appears on 304 paths (19.1%), allowing projects with multiple eligible accounts to distribute requests across their separate allowances. Our policy analysis finds that 58 of the 62 services with available terms explicitly prohibit at least one examined practice, including automated access, account sharing, or service redistribution. These restrictions cover both downstream API provision and the means of obtaining upstream inference. Our findings inform defenses against unauthorized consumption and redistribution of free LLM resources. We have responsibly disclosed our findings to the relevant providers.

Title: A comprehensive survey of deep face verification systems adversarial attacks and defense strategies

  • Authors: Sohair Kilany, Ahmed Mahfouz
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33231
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33231
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33231]] A comprehensive survey of deep face verification systems adversarial attacks and defense strategies(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33231)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Face Verification (FV) systems have exhibited remarkable performance in verification tasks and have consequently garnered extensive adoption across various applications, from identity duplication to authentication in mobile payments. However, the surge in popularity of face verification has raised concerns about potential vulnerabilities in the face of adversarial attacks. These concerns originate from the fact that advanced FV systems, which rely on deep neural networks, have recently demonstrated susceptibility to crafted input samples known as adversarial examples. Although imperceptible to human observers, adversarial examples can deceive deep neural networks during the testing and deployment phases. These vulnerabilities raised significant concerns about the deployment of deep neural networks in safety-critical contexts, prompting extensive investigations into adversarial attacks and corresponding defense strategies. This comprehensive survey provides a comprehensive overview of recent advances in deep face verification, encompassing a broad spectrum of topics such as algorithmic designs, database utilization, protocols, and application scenarios. Furthermore, we conduct an in-depth examination of state-of-the-art algorithms to generate adversarial examples and the defense mechanisms devised to mitigate such adversarial threats.

Title: How Many Samples Is RL Worth? Best-of-N versus RL Post-Training for Text-to-Image Models

  • Authors: Salil Bhatnagar
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33232
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33232
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33232]] How Many Samples Is RL Worth? Best-of-N versus RL Post-Training for Text-to-Image Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33232)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) on a reward that can be checked automatically has become a common last step in the training of text-to-image models. A simpler way to get more correct images is best-of-N: draw N images from the base model and keep the one that a picker prefers. We ask how many base model samples one RL sample is worth, and call this number the exchange rate. We measure it for Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium and its public Flow-GRPO checkpoint on 398 new prompts in the style of GenEval, the benchmark behind the RL reward, with human labels on every prompt and two automatic judges other than the reward. The answer depends on the prompt. Four fifths of the prompts do not ask for a position (for example, one object left of another). On these, the base model catches up: best-of-16 with an off-the-shelf vision-language model (VLM) as the picker is about as good as one RL image. A person who looks through up to 3 base images finds a correct one as often as one RL image is correct. On the prompts that ask for a position, RL is far ahead: a person finds a correct image among 8 base images for only 52% of the prompts, against 92% for one RL image. Over all prompts, our primary test, which we fixed in advance, finds best-of-16 with the VLM picker 9.3 points behind one RL image when both automatic judges must pass an image. People see a smaller gap, 5.5 and 6.5 points in two checks. FLUX.1-dev shows a similar gap in the same test. RL also solves 20 prompts that the base model misses in 128 tries, and 1,024 tries recover half of them. The automatic judges are wrong in opposite directions: a detector-based judge like the reward, as used in our primary test, reports a larger gap than people see, and VLM judges report little or none. Comparisons of RL with best-of-N should therefore check the selected images by hand.

Title: Resource-Efficient Multimodal Fusion for Early Rice Blast Forecasting in Sustainable Agriculture

  • Authors: Shubhajyoti Das, Pritam Bikram, Arindam Biswas, Samrat Paul, Parimal Sinha, Bhargab B. Bhattacharya
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33237
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33237
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33237]] Resource-Efficient Multimodal Fusion for Early Rice Blast Forecasting in Sustainable Agriculture(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33237)
  • Keywords: security, transformer
  • Abstract: Resource-efficient artificial intelligence has become increasingly important for sustainable agriculture, where timely disease forecasting must be achieved under computational and energy constraints. Early estimation of rice blast can enhance food security by optimizing crop management practices while reducing excessive pesticide use. However, existing frameworks often suffer from high computational overhead and limited capability to model the complex spectral-temporal dependencies present in heterogeneous meteorological and remote sensing data. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a lightweight multimodal fusion framework comprising an attentive positional encoding-based Transformer and a convolutional neural network (CNN)-embedded gated recurrent unit (GRU) for efficient spectral-temporal representation learning. The attention-enhanced positional encoding assigns adaptive importance to sequential meteorological tokens, enabling effective modelling of temporal characteristics. In parallel, the lightweight CNN extracts discriminative spectral features, while the GRU captures temporal dynamics from remote sensing imagery acquired over two time-instances. A multimodal fusion layer integrates these heterogeneous representations to forecast rice blast risk 5-10 days before symptom manifestation across diverse agro-climatic zones. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed framework outperforms state-of-the-art methods, achieving a validation accuracy of 96.93% and an F1-score of 95.79%. In addition to its predictive performance, the lightweight multimodal architecture substantially reduces computational complexity, inference latency, and carbon emissions, making it an energy-efficient solution for large-scale disease forecasting. The proposed framework provides a practical foundation for resource-efficient artificial intelligence in sustainable agriculture.

Title: Sequential Testing on Complex and High Dimensional Data Streams Leveraging Unsupervised Model Errors

  • Authors: George Stamatelis, Iordanis Koutsopoulos
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33238
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33238
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33238]] Sequential Testing on Complex and High Dimensional Data Streams Leveraging Unsupervised Model Errors(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33238)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: We propose two general, distribution-free test statistics for binary Sequential Hypothesis Testing (SHT) and Sequential Change Point Detection (SCPD) respectively, in streaming environments where the data (e.g., network traffic metadata) are too complicated for the exact probabilistic modeling required by conventional likelihood ratio-driven tests. Our framework also addresses the challenge of information asymmetry, where data is available only from the null/pre-change regime and not from the alternative/post-change one such as zero-day attacks or emerging disease related regimes. The proposed statistics monitor the errors of a predictive or reconstructive machine learning model trained solely on null/pre-change data, relying on imperfect generalization (the model's performance will degrade when deployed on data from the alternative/post-change class). Under mild assumptions on the model's errors, we establish rigorous analytical guarantees: strict non-asymptotic error control for SHT, and explicit bounds on the Average Run Length (ARL) and the Expected Detection Delay (EDD) for SCPD. Extensive evaluations in target tracking, chaotic dynamical systems, and real-world network security datasets, demonstrate that our framework consistently satisfies prescribed constraints where existing algorithms succumb to inflated missed detections and/or false alarms. Finally, because our methods operate on scalar errors without requiring online retraining or kernel evaluations, they are highly scalable.

Title: Beyond the Efficiency-Efficacy Dilemma: A Critical Analysis of Transferability in Adversarial Malware Detection

  • Authors: Veysel Alevcan, Mohammad Furqan Ali, Teresa Sousa, Luís Miguel Campos, Christoforos Ntantogian
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33243
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33243
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33243]] Beyond the Efficiency-Efficacy Dilemma: A Critical Analysis of Transferability in Adversarial Malware Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33243)
  • Keywords: security, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Machine Learning (ML) models are already being deployed at scale for malware detection. Therefore there is a pressing need to rigorously study adversarial robustness. To that end, we introduce the Adversarial AI Engine (AAIE): a platform for automated security stress-testing. We performed transfer attacks, via EMBER2018, against a Random Forest target. Counterintuitively, we found single-step FGSM to have the highest attack success rate (ASR) of 52.9% compared to iterative PGD with 45.2% (p < 0.002). Compared to iterative attacks which tend to overfit surrogate networks creating brittle perturbations, FGSM created more transferable examples. We benchmarked our attacks using ASR, transferability score, evasion rate, and resilience index. Through these metrics, proposed in concordance with emerging standards in AI governance, we expose shortcomings as our transferability scores peak at ~0.8 with constrained perturbations. We highlight the practical security implications of these findings and demonstrate AAIE for automated adversarial auditing.

Title: MonoQPM: Splitting Features into Concepts for Inherent Interpretability and Predictive Performance

  • Authors: Thomas Norrenbrock, Robert Zimmermann, Timo Kaiser, Sovan Biswas, Neslihan Kose, Bodo Rosenhahn
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33252
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33252
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33252]] MonoQPM: Splitting Features into Concepts for Inherent Interpretability and Predictive Performance(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33252)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Alongside superposition, polysemanticity is one of the primary obstacles to achieving interpretable deep neural networks. While these phenomena are typically intrinsically linked, the Quadratic Programming Enhanced Model (QPM) naturally decouples them. By representing classes with a sparse binary assignment of very few features, QPM prevents superposition by design on its final features but still exhibits polysemanticity. However, measuring polysemanticity is an open problem. This work proposes a utility-focused approach to measuring polysemanticity by quantifying the decrease in interference-induced activation errors, which yields the practical utility of tighter prediction sets using Conformal Prediction (CP). We explicitly disentangle the polysemantic features of QPM into monosemantic concepts to create the Monosemantic QPM (MonoQPM). Because its features are disentangled, MonoQPM acts as a significantly more efficient Conformal Predictor. Additionally, we introduce CUBCars, a dataset providing ground truth information about shared concepts. Using this and other datasets, we demonstrate that polysemanticity emerges in QPM across all tested architectures, but is effectively alleviated by MonoQPM. For instance, MonoQPM guarantees 88% coverage using Adaptive Prediction Sets on ImageNet with just 66% of the frozen QPM's set size.

Title: Onnx2TEE: Automated Graph-Aware Model Partitioning for TEE-based Edge AI Protection

  • Authors: Daniela M. Vela Hernandez, Samuel Hildebrand, Sean Oesch, Brian Weber, Shayan Monadjemi
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.ET
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33262
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33262
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33262]] Onnx2TEE: Automated Graph-Aware Model Partitioning for TEE-based Edge AI Protection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33262)
  • Keywords: protect, segmentation
  • Abstract: AI models are increasingly being deployed at the edge. Since these models often require significant research and development to produce, are often deployed in potentially hostile environments, and downstream systems often rely on accurate data; protecting them from theft or manipulation is worthy of serious consideration. Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs), which provide hardware-enforced separation of software execution, can protect model weights but impose strict resource constraints, making full-model execution impractical for large neural networks. We present Onnx2TEE, an automated, model-independent pipeline that partitions ONNX models across rich, trusted execution environments for non-sequential feed-forward networks using graph-aware segmentation to preserve computational dependencies while minimizing boundary communication and reducing TEE-induced inference latency. Evaluation on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin using an Arm TrustZone TEE demonstrates that model parameters can be highly concentrated in individual segments, supporting selective protection of parameter-dense model components. The evaluation further shows that the generated trusted app's memory requirements depend on both computation and intermediate tensor sizes.

Title: FrIdéo: French News Outlet Ideology Scoring via Multi-Source Evidence Fusion and Distancing-Aware Lexical Scoring

  • Authors: Amr Sobhy
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33266
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33266
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33266]] FrIdéo: French News Outlet Ideology Scoring via Multi-Source Evidence Fusion and Distancing-Aware Lexical Scoring(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33266)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Outlet ideology is a standard explanatory variable in media framing, media effects, and political communication research, but continuous, ecosystem-wide coverage lets it enter as graded exposure rather than a coarse bin. For France, existing references are either broad but non-analytic or analytic but sparse and source-specific, and none we are aware of offers ecosystem-wide coverage on a unified continuous scale. We contribute FrIdéo: to our knowledge the first ecosystem-wide continuous ideology scores for the French press, a transparent composite score for 30 French news outlets, fusing nine independent families of evidence with empirical-Bayes-style shrinkage toward a structural prior. For the text-based family we introduce symmetric plain/qualified scoring, which separates the plain use of ideologically loaded vocabulary from its qualified (distanced) use on both sides, a distinction bag-of-words methods miss. The resulting scale runs from L'Humanité on the far left to Valeurs actuelles on the far right; the far-right pole is near-indistinguishable and CNews ties Le Figaro. Because the text family reads current articles, it also captures editorial change that static sources miss, such as the Journal du Dimanche's sharp rightward shift after its 2023 ownership change. The ordering is robust to perturbation and to dropping any single family, and agrees with held-out Media Bias Fact Check labels. We release the scores, confidence intervals, and replication code.

Title: Two Timescales of Forgetting in Fine-tuning of Language Models: Fast Behavioral Suppression and Slow Representational Drift

  • Authors: Yilin Luo, Jin Hwa Lee, Andrew M. Saxe, Antonio Sclocchi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cond-mat.dis-nn, cs.CL, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33302
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33302
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33302]] Two Timescales of Forgetting in Fine-tuning of Language Models: Fast Behavioral Suppression and Slow Representational Drift(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33302)
  • Keywords: transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Today, a core pipeline of artificial intelligence systems is pretraining on a broad range of text corpora and then fine-tuning on specific tasks or desired behaviors. In particular, it remains unclear whether surface behavioral suppression happens together with the erasure of latent representations that support it. In this work, we study supervised fine-tuning (SFT) by pretraining transformers on a controlled context-free grammar with hierarchical latent structure, the Random Hierarchy Model (RHM), and fine-tuning with a restricted subset of its generative rules. Finally, we observe analogous results in a realistic setup with natural language, suggesting that this timescale separation in forgetting is a more general phenomenon of fine-tuning language models.

Title: StyleID++: Position-Decoupled and Structure-Coherent Style Retrieval across Diffusion Architectures

  • Authors: Chun Xie, Itaru Kitahara
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33323
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33323
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33323]] StyleID++: Position-Decoupled and Structure-Coherent Style Retrieval across Diffusion Architectures(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33323)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Attention injection offers a remarkably simple route to training-free style transfer: content queries retrieve style keys and values inside a pretrained diffusion model. We show that this operation also creates an overlooked failure mode. Position signals that are useful within one image become a spurious shared coordinate system across two spatially unrelated images, causing content tokens to favor style tokens at similar coordinates. This cross-image positional leakage appears in both convolutional U-Nets and diffusion transformers (DiTs), despite their different position mechanisms. Removing the positional shortcut exposes a second problem: unconstrained global retrieval lets related content tokens select incompatible style regions, producing fragmented appearance. We present StyleID++, a training-free framework that addresses the two problems jointly. Position-Decoupled Retrieval reduces coordinate-dependent matching through circular padding in U-Nets and injection-only RoPE removal in DiTs, while preserving native position modeling elsewhere. Affinity-Coherent Refinement then regularizes style assignments with the content self-attention graph, encouraging structurally related tokens to retrieve compatible style evidence. We quantify the leakage with a coordinate--centroid correlation and a permutation test on the style reference. The permutation error can be driven to zero in both backbones, which identifies the position mechanism as the carrier of the leakage, and StyleID++ obtains the best ArtFID of all methods we compare.

Title: The Marauder's Map: Bézier Manifolds Reveal Hidden Surfaces for Model Merging and Ensembling

  • Authors: Abhiram Iyer, Mark T. Harnett, Sarthak Chandra
  • Subjects: cs.LG, q-bio.NC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33353
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33353
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33353]] The Marauder's Map: Bézier Manifolds Reveal Hidden Surfaces for Model Merging and Ensembling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33353)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Neural circuits are famously more robust than deep networks. This is often explained through degeneracy, where structurally distinct configurations support the same function. However, real biological systems exhibit a more flexible form of degeneracy in which specialized circuits continually adapt towards new competencies through localized changes while preserving existing function. Deep learning studies degeneracy through mode connectivity, which connects same-task networks by low-loss curves, sheets, or volumes. However, the field lacks a counterpart to flexible degeneracy, which demands more than ordinary mode connectivity. Defining such an analogue requires asking whether many independently initialized models trained on distinct tasks can be connected by a high-dimensional region of multi-task solutions. The resulting manifold forms a navigable "Marauder's Map" that connects specialist models through a shared geometry of competent solutions. Trained across vision and language domains, $\textit{Nimbus}$ produces manifolds that are functionally and locally weight-flat: ensembles sampled from them degrade more gracefully than baselines under input and weight perturbations, with the largest gains in NLL loss and calibration error. $\textit{Nimbus}$ supplies a deep learning interpretation of flexible degeneracy, recasting model connectivity from a low-dimensional path between distant solutions into a high-dimensional volume of nearby ones.

To reduce this cost, we propose an automated policy evolution framework that iteratively refines the memory construction and retrieval policies around a fixed pretrained decision model. The resulting method, DeLoVi-E, maintains comparable performance while reducing input tokens by 34x on average compared with DeLoVi-S.   • Abstract: In this paper, we synchronize multiple videos of the same dynamic event through object relationships. We thus propose to bring back the traditional wisdom that objects are reliable cues for global reasoning about cameras. Measured in 3D, the relationships between objects, such as the distance between two people, are stable across all views up to scale; they can serve as cues for alignment. We obtain these identities by giving a vision-language model a few frames of every camera and prompting it to identify the objects in the scene. Where depth or segmentation fails, we complement these 3D cues with 2D motion from point tracks. Finally, we synchronize the cameras by finding the temporal offsets at which the cues of different cameras agree best, first for each camera pair and then for all cameras jointly with robust optimization. We evaluate our method on four standard benchmarks, outperforming the state of the art.

Title: Sequentially Acquiring Concept Knowledge to Guide Continual Learning

  • Authors: Shivanand Kundargi, Kowshik Thopalli, Tejas Gokhale
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33399
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33399
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33399]] Sequentially Acquiring Concept Knowledge to Guide Continual Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33399)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: The goal of continual learning (CL) is to adapt to new data (plasticity) while retaining the knowledge acquired from old data (stability). Existing methods are explicitly designed to focus on balancing stability and plasticity to mitigate the challenge of catastrophic forgetting while promoting learning. However, the impact of order and nature of samples from the new data that the network is trained on remains an under explored factor. We argue that a CL algorithm should have the ability to rank incoming samples in terms of their relationship with prior data and their effect on the learning process. In this work, we investigate if scoring and prioritizing incoming data based on their semantic relationships with the model's current knowledge can benefit CL. We propose SACK, short for Sequentially Acquiring Concept Knowledge, a plug and play technique for continual learning methods. SACK dissects categorical knowledge of the model into fine-grained concepts, computes the relationships between previously learned concepts and new concepts in each experience, and uses this relationship knowledge for prioritizing new samples. Experiments across several types of CL methods (regularization, replay, and prompt based) in class incremental and task incremental settings demonstrate that integration of SACK not only improves accuracy and reduces forgetting but also handles long tail distribution, helps focus on semantically interpretable regions, and yields better calibrated and robust models compared to prior approaches.

  • Keywords: generative, segmentation
  • Abstract: An RGB-D camera that films an object being moved, often from one viewpoint, sees one side of it and how its parts move. We evaluate on Articulate-100 and on HArt, a new benchmark of 60 PartNet-Mobility objects and 8 robotic hands and grippers rendered as RGB-D videos with ground-truth parts and joints. ArtKit reduces the axis and part motion errors of the strongest baseline by more than half on the objects, as well as the axis error on the hands. ArtKit also runs on real recordings and generated videos.

In this work, we study whether amortized adversarial generation can provide better initializations for gradient-based attacks.   • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Flow matching has shown strong potential in generative tasks, while its optimization via Reinforcement Learning (RL) remains underexplored, especially in continuous-discrete mixed settings.   • Authors: Ishita Chakraborty, Xingjian You, Shrabastee Banerjee

  • Authors: Yi He

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33469
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33469

  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) in personalized services often relies on sensitive interaction data. This setting poses two key challenges: past trajectories are repeatedly reused for policy evaluation, and reward choices may depend on previously released private statistics. The proposed algorithm combines batched proximal policy optimization (PPO) updates with optimistic policy evaluation, using private regression statistics and reward estimates to protect both trajectories and reward functions under joint differential privacy (JDP). The additional privacy terms scale as $\widetilde{O} \left(K^{5/8}\right)$ and $\widetilde{O} \left(K^{1/2}\right)$. Thus, privacy incurs only lower-order additive costs in $K$, even under adaptive adversarial rewards.

Title: Merkle Trees Minimize Verifier Complexity in the ROM

  • Authors: Miranda Christ, Oliver Korten, Tal Malkin, Noam Mazor, Shyamal Patel
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33508
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33508
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33508]] Merkle Trees Minimize Verifier Complexity in the ROM(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33508)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Nearly five decades after their introduction, Merkle trees remain the most efficient hash-based vector commitments. They compress a vector $x \in \{0,1\}^n$ into a commitment whose size is independent of $n$, such that a verifier can open any bit $x_i$ using $\log n$ hash evaluations. The security of the scheme (intractability of finding two distinct openings for a given bit using the same commitment) follows from the collision resistance of the hash function. The verifier's runtime complexity is a bottleneck in many key applications of vector commitments, including SNARKs, credentials, and authenticated dictionaries. As such, a longstanding open question has been whether it is possible to construct hash-based vector commitments where the verifier makes a sublogarithmic number of hash evaluations. We prove that sublogarithmic verifier complexity is impossible to achieve through black-box use of a hash function; we not only rule out constructions from collision resistant hash functions, but in fact show that commitment schemes with sublogarithmic verifier complexity are unachievable in the random oracle model. In particular, any vector commitment with deterministic verifiers has verifier query complexity $\Omega_{\theta}(\log n)$, where $\theta$ is the input length of the random oracle. This bound is matched up to constant factors by high-arity Merkle trees. The key technical lemma in our proof is a disjoint product lemma, which can be viewed as a computational analog of the van den Berg-Kesten-Reimer (BKR) inequality. We believe this lemma is of independent interest, and in particular may be useful for showing other lower bounds and separations in the random oracle model.

Title: PRECEPT: Security Self-Improvement of Coding Agents via Evolving Principles

  • Authors: Keyan Guo, Rupam Patir, Haipeng Cai, Hongxin Hu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33519
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33519
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33519]] PRECEPT: Security Self-Improvement of Coding Agents via Evolving Principles(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33519)
  • Keywords: secure, security
  • Abstract: AI coding agents increasingly write and submit code with limited human review, and much of it passes functional tests while remaining insecure. We show that these failures persist. Across 14 task sequences, Codex, Claude Code, and Qwen Code fixed a vulnerability 85.7% of the time when shown the failing security test, then reintroduced the same vulnerability class in a new session 26.7% of the time, about as often as agents that had never seen it (27.5%). The capability is present; the retention is not. Neither recording the repair in the agent's persistent context nor supplying published secure coding principles closed the gap: the recorded fix stayed tied to the family of the task it came from, and the principles, written for human developers, remained too general to apply to the code at hand. The knowledge exists; it is not activated. We call the missing ability security self-improvement: an agent learns from its own security failures so that each lesson persists across sessions, applies to new code, and does not break what works. We present PRECEPT, which equips coding agents with this ability through an evolving library that starts from authoritative secure coding principles and revises them against the agent's own failures, so that each principle comes to fire on the code where that agent actually goes wrong. A revision is kept only if fresh executions show it makes code more secure while keeping functional success within a small tolerance of the agent without the library. Improvement continues across evolution steps rather than saturating after the first. On task families never seen during evolution, PRECEPT's weakest agent reaches 97.1% secure working code, above the strongest unaided agent at 81.9% and the strongest baseline at 87.7%.

Title: A Perturbation-Driven Framework for Sensitivity Analysis of Dimensionality Reduction

  • Authors: Md Afridi Hasan, Jaemin Jo, Takanori Fujiwara
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.HC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33523
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33523

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Low-dimensional representations produced by dimensionality reduction (DR) are commonly evaluated using distortion measures such as trustworthiness and continuity. In this paper, we study the sensitivity of DR methods to plausible input variations by quantifying changes in structural preservation and visually diagnosing how these changes manifest in the resulting representations. This perspective reflects the fact that real-world datasets are finite, noisy samples of an underlying distribution, and DR should not produce substantially different structures under small data changes. We introduce a model-agnostic, perturbation-driven sensitivity evaluation framework that treats perturbations as controlled probes of plausible data variation. Across multiple perturbation families and strength levels, we repeatedly perturb the input, apply a DR method, and evaluate the resulting low-dimensional representations using a set of distortion measures to derive sensitivity profiles for each DR method. We demonstrate the utility of our framework through case studies on DR method comparison, behavior analysis, and robustness improvement. We find that methods performing well according to conventional distortion measures (e.g., t-SNE) can still exhibit substantial sensitivity, leading to instability in visually salient structures such as trajectories and clusters, while certain methods (e.g., multidimensional scaling) may exhibit improved structural preservation under specific perturbations. These results highlight the importance of sensitivity analysis for comparing DR methods, diagnosing perturbation-sensitive visual patterns, and guiding the development of more robust DR methods toward trustworthy visual analytics.

Title: AVERT: Anomalous Value Erasure with Response Testing against Visual Prompt Injection

  • Authors: Newt Hue-Nam K. Nguyen, Jui-Yu Ho, Wei-Han Chen, Pin-Yen Ting, Yangqiu Song, Shang-Tse Chen, Jun-Cheng Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33532
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33532
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33532]] AVERT: Anomalous Value Erasure with Response Testing against Visual Prompt Injection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33532)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, diffusion
  • Abstract: Vision Language Models (VLMs) are vulnerable to visual prompt injection, in which an adversarially perturbed image forces the model to respond with the attacker's chosen content, such as malicious tool calls, website links, or advertisements, sometimes regardless of the user's question. Existing defenses, such as diffusion-based purification and image-token masking, mitigate these attacks at a substantial cost to the model's utility on benign inputs. To address this challenge, we propose AVERT, which removes adversarial feature directions only when the model's answer depends on them. Our key procedures involve identifying anomalous value subspaces and detecting answer dependence. To identify the anomalous value subspace, we compare the statistics of each input's attention-head outputs with those of clean images and extract the directions along which the input deviates the most. We then project out these directions to remove the injected content. Because the same directions also carry benign visual information, we apply the removal only to answers that depend on them, namely answers whose likelihood collapses once the directions are removed or that are as likely under unrelated questions as under the user's question. Against white-box attacks on four VLMs (Qwen2-VL, Qwen2.5-VL, LLaVA, InternVL2.5) and four injection benchmarks, AVERT lowers average attack success from 86-90% to at most 0.6% while retaining at least 96% of undefended utility averaged over thirteen benchmarks. Its defense-utility F-score ($F_{DU}$), the harmonic mean of attacks stopped and utility retained, is 98.0-99.1 and the highest among all compared defenses on every model.

Title: Trigger-Invariant Contrastive Unlearning for Backdoor Removal

  • Authors: Santosh Chapagain, Shah Muhammad Hamdi, Soukaina Filali Boubrahimi
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33571
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33571

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Backdoor attacks pose a severe threat to modern NLP models by embedding hidden triggers that induce malicious behavior at inference time. Recent unlearning methods such as gradient ascent (GA) attempt to remove backdoors by maximizing the loss on poisoned samples; however, these methods can suffer from a critical limitation: they may shift the trigger's influence to other classes rather than eliminating it. In this work, we propose Trigger-Invariant Contrastive Unlearning (TICU), a framework for mitigating textual backdoor behavior by enforcing prediction and representation invariance to trigger insertion. Title: Amortizing Exact Orthogonality: Drift-Triggered Restoration for Orthogonal Fine-Tuning

  • Authors: Lanxin Zhao (1), Xiaoyu Li (2), Dai Shi (3), Junbin Gao (1), Andi Han (1) ((1) The University of Sydney, (2) University of New South Wales, (3) University of Cambridge)
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33589
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33589
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33589]] Amortizing Exact Orthogonality: Drift-Triggered Restoration for Orthogonal Fine-Tuning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33589)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Orthogonal finetuning (OFT) adapts a pretrained weight by an orthogonal transform and preserves its singular values and row angles, but existing methods construct an exact or approximate orthogonal transform at every optimization step. We measure how far a transform $A$ is from orthogonal by its orthogonality defect $\|A^\top A-I\|_2$. For OFTv2, which truncates a Cayley-Neumann series, we show that the truncation order does not determine the defect. At its default order, block defects in checkpoints from the reference implementation span five orders of magnitude. We propose Restarted Tangent Orthogonal Finetuning (RTOFT), which uses the first-order update $W(I+S)$ of a weight $W$ with a skew-symmetric $S$ and restores exact orthogonality by a polar decomposition only when the drift $\|S\|_2$ reaches a tolerance $\tau$. The defect of $I+S$ is exactly $\|S\|_2^2$. Therefore, reading the drift after every update and restoring before the next forward pass guarantees a defect of at most $\tau^2$ in every forward pass, independently of the loss, data, model, and optimizer. A final restoration makes the returned transform exactly orthogonal. On GLUE, RTOFT reduces the training time of block-diagonal, subspace, and butterfly parameterizations from $1.10$-$1.58\times$ to $0.97$-$1.13\times$ that of LoRA, and its block-diagonal version exceeds LoRA by $1.98\pm0.39$ points on the six-task average over five seeds. At $\tau=0.10$, the largest drift read during training stays between $0.10$ and $0.12$ across DeBERTaV3, Qwen, and diffusion models, whereas it ranges from $0.17$ to $1.65$ when restoring once per epoch. In all seven settings of our ablation, the tangent update is worse without restoration than with it, and restoring after every step yields no measurable accuracy gain over restoring at the tolerance and costs $4.5$-$27.5\%$ more per step.

Title: Small Leaks Sink Great Ships: Automated Analysis of Protocols That Tolerate Low-Entropy Keys

  • Authors: Yongkang Xiao, Jing Chen, Min Shi, Kun He, Mei Wang, Ruiying Du
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33595
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33595

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Modern cryptographic protocols such as WPA2-PSK, Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE), and Bluetooth Classic (BC) tolerate low-entropy keys for user-friendliness, backward compatibility, or resource-constrained deployment, yet existing symbolic protocol verifiers rely on the perfect-encryption assumption and therefore cannot reason about brute-force attackers. Prior work addresses this gap only through protocol-specific, manually encoded reveal rules, which neither generalize across protocols nor systematically explore compound-key decomposition. We introduce the first protocol-agnostic, automated symbolic methodology for low-entropy keys, built on three abstractions: the message-derivation fact MDerive, which captures brute-force prerequisites independently of concrete function symbols; a three-tier breaking-oracle design (value-breaking, relationship-breaking, message-derivation) that composes with the standard Dolev-Yao attacker; and a graded bit-level attacker model that bounds the key length an attacker is allowed to exhaust. Across three real-world protocols and six BC device configurations, our results show that entropy-aware symbolic analysis is both feasible and necessary.

Title: State Space Attention: Learning Context-Dependent Memory Retention

  • Authors: Ege Çakar, Cengiz Pehlevan

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33625

  • Abstract: Standard attention's key-value cache grows linearly with context length. Bounding it raises the question of what a sequence model should retain for long-context reasoning. A learned scorer rescores every chunk against newly arriving context and the rest of memory. A fixed-context Transformer is then the special case that always keeps the most recent tokens. The largest remaining gap to full attention at small language model scale is on RULER. SSA makes a fixed number of tokens useful over a longer context, and since it reads memory with standard attention, we propose that it can be stacked with KV cache eviction methods. We also pretrain and post-train 400M-parameter models and measure how SSA's training and inference costs scale with context length and model size. Together, these experiments examine the capabilities and limitations of learned memory retention for long-context sequence modeling.

Title: Verifiable but Not Predictable: What Sentence Embeddings Know About Propositional Decomposition

  • Authors: Qingan Li, Tianxiao Wu, Xiling Cui
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33668
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33668
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33668]] Verifiable but Not Predictable: What Sentence Embeddings Know About Propositional Decomposition(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33668)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Quality control for proposition decomposition by large language models (LLMs) raises two asymmetric questions. After decomposition, one can verify: given a sentence and a candidate proposition, does the proposition belong to that sentence? Before decomposition, one would like to predict: how many propositions should the sentence yield? On PropSegmEnt, a corpus of English Wikipedia sentences each segmented by three human annotators, we find that the two questions live in different representational spaces. A natural language inference (NLI) judge verifies membership with 92.8% accuracy and an area under the ROC curve (AUC) of 0.977, and its residual errors turn out to be mostly propositions that are true in document context rather than genuine entailment mistakes. Vector geometry cannot verify: cosine similarity saturates, and a sentence embedding is not a linear combination of its propositions' embeddings. For prediction, every symbolic anchor we test, namely token count, the CPIDR idea-density counter, and a Neo-Davidsonian predicate count, reduces to a length proxy once token count is controlled. A linear ridge probe on sentence embeddings is the only predictor that keeps genuine count signal: its length-partialled correlation with human counts is 0.40-0.49 with bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs) excluding zero, and it orders length-matched sentence pairs correctly 67-71% of the time against a 50% chance level, statistically matching the inter-annotator agreement ceiling. This signal survives INT8 quantization without loss, INT4 at a small cost, and Matryoshka truncation to 256 dimensions, and it works as well in a 2021 encoder as in a 2025 one. Its structured weakness is a systematic under-splitting of long sentences. We distill the protocol lessons, including a retracted pilot study, into a checklist for probing length-confounded quantities.

Title: Rethinking Knowledge Distillation for Diffusion Language Models

  • Authors: Yuxuan Sun, Yuanjian Xu, Jianing Hao, Yu Li, Sowmen Das, Zhong Li, Sangwoong Yoon, Miguel R. D. Rodrigues

  • Abstract: Knowledge distillation has recently proven highly effective for improving autoregressive models, but its applicability to DLMs remains poorly understood. Title: Node Differential Privacy via Node-Stable Graph Projections

  • Authors: Ishaq Aden-Ali, Adam Sealfon, Adam Smith, Thomas Steinke, Jonathan Ullman
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.DS, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33672
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33672
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33672]] Node Differential Privacy via Node-Stable Graph Projections(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33672)
  • Keywords: privacy, protect
  • Abstract: Node-differentially private algorithms enable learning on network-structured datasets in which records reflect interactions among individuals. They protect the privacy of each individual's information, including all their associated records. A key challenge in the design of such algorithms is to exploit the structure of realistic graphs---where most nodes have low degree---to achieve high accuracy, without compromising on the worst-case privacy guarantee. In this work we give two new tools for designing accurate, computationally efficient node-differentially private algorithms. First, we give a nearly linear time, randomized, node-stable graph projection: the projection leaves graphs of maximum degree $D$ unchanged, maps arbitrary graphs to subgraphs of maximum degree $\hat D \approx D$, and ensures that changing one node of the input graph has only a small effect on the distribution of the edges selected among the remaining vertices. Unlike prior approaches, which abstain when the input violates the degree bound, our projection acts as degree clipping: even when $D$ is set well below the true maximum degree (trading truncation bias at high-degree outliers for lower privacy noise), it retains at least as many edges as a maximal $D$-bounded subgraph. Second, for graphs with bounded edge weights, we give a deterministic nearly linear time Lipschitz extension for the total edge weight that agrees with the original sum on graphs of maximum degree $D$ yet is $D$-Lipschitz on all graphs. Finally, we show how to apply these methods to design node-private algorithms for high-dimensional vector summation and gradient descent on datasets where records are associated with graph edges, such as email threads.

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: The Locate-Then-Edit framework enables efficient knowledge updates in large language models without requiring full retraining. Recent research has increasingly focused on sequential editing, where knowledge arrives continuously and the model is updated incrementally. However, existing sequential methods commonly suffer from catastrophic forgetting and model collapse after only a few hundred edits. In contrast, batch editing - which jointly optimizes all edits and yields the optimal solution under a specified editing objective - can support tens of thousands of edits with high edit efficacy while preserving the model's general capabilities, resulting in a substantial performance gap between the two editing paradigms. Title: Understanding Mode Collapse in Diffusion Models under Classifier-Free Guidance

  • Authors: Daniel Pfrommer, Chenyang Yuan, Frank Permenter, Ali Jadbabaie
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33719
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33719
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33719]] Understanding Mode Collapse in Diffusion Models under Classifier-Free Guidance(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33719)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: The use of classifier-free guidance in diffusion and flow models has proven critical to their empirical success, as it allows for the controllable generation of lower-temperature and higher-fidelity samples. Despite its widespread use, classifier-free guidance remains both empirically and theoretically poorly understood, owing to the difficulty of solving the associated transport equations for arbitrary normalizing flows. In this paper, we observe that the effects of classifier-free guidance arise from a mismatch between the representation capabilities of the conditional and unconditional distributions, wherein the conditional distribution exhibits higher complexity and fidelity. We propose an analytical model to capture this observation: the conditional distribution is a Gaussian mixture whose means lie on an ellipsoid, and the unconditional distribution is a single Gaussian approximating the mixture. The flows arising from this mixture are closed under scaling, enabling us to derive analytic closed-form solutions for the guided distribution at all noise scales and guidance levels. Our analytic solution shows that classifier-free guidance induces (a) a shift along a mean-axis direction and (b) a persistent skew toward, and concentration around, a singular point corresponding to the conditional sample starting from zero noise. We validate our theoretical predictions on pretrained, large-scale image diffusion models.

We introduce VAPOR (Visual Attention Persistence with On-demand Refresh), a training-free framework that reduces this redundancy by reusing visual attention outputs across decoding steps and refreshing them when needed. Experiments on eight image and four video reasoning benchmarks across two MLLM families show that VAPOR improves accuracy over dense attention on nearly all benchmarks while achieving 1.11-1.21x decoding speedups. Compared with the strongest state-of-the-art acceleration baseline in each setting, VAPOR further improves accuracy by 0.35-2.15 percentage points while achieving comparable or lower decoding latency.   • Abstract: Masked diffusion models (MDMs) have shown strong performance on structured reasoning tasks such as Sudoku, Trip Planning, and Countdown. However, simple examples seem to illustrate that resolving a token's dependencies before unmasking it should be important. Therefore, we examine whether this principle, which we call dependency-ready decoding, would contribute to better accuracy, from a practical and a theoretical perspective. We examine a recent decoder that implements dependency-readiness through attention, and generally find that when it performs better than confidence-based or future utility baselines, its decoding order collapses to left-to-right decoding. For theoretical analysis, we establish a definition of dependency without resorting to attention, measured by an information-theoretic score of how much revealing a single masked token (or a set) would shift the model's prediction for another. We prove that when the margin is large relative to this score, no such revelation can change the top prediction, so in this regime Maximum-Margin decoding already commits dependency-ready tokens. These findings leave only a narrow scope for future dependency-ready decoders: their potential benefit is limited to positions with margins smaller than the information-theoretic sensitivity, and reliance on attention should be questioned.

Title: MotionODE: Enhancing Autoregressive Text-to-Motion Generation with Continuous-Depth Modeling

  • Authors: Zhaoxin Fan, Jian Yang, Kejian Wu, Zeming Liu, Wenjun Wu, Lili Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33826
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33826
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33826]] MotionODE: Enhancing Autoregressive Text-to-Motion Generation with Continuous-Depth Modeling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33826)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Autoregressive text-to-motion generation requires local motion predictions to reconcile cross-temporal textual requirements with the available motion context. While existing methods primarily improve motion representations and generation order, the internal formation of conditioned motion representations remains less explored. We introduce MotionODE, a continuous-depth post-training framework that refines hidden-state evolution in pretrained autoregressive generators. MotionODE reinterprets selected Transformer residual branches as local vector fields and replaces their original updates with midpoint Runge--Kutta (RK2) integration, while preserving the motion representation and outer generation mechanism. To address the mismatch between pretrained update paths and newly introduced intermediate states, we propose mixed one-step/two-step RK2 post-training under motion-token supervision, with optional distillation and update-map constraints to preserve pretrained behavior. This minimally invasive framework supports both next-token and next-scale generation without training the generator from scratch. Extensive experiments on HumanML3D demonstrate consistent improvements in text--motion alignment across MoScale, BAMM and LMR, highlighting internal representation updates as a complementary direction for improving autoregressive motion generation.

Title: FEAST: Fair and Efficient Adaptive Sampling for Task-Free On-Policy Learning

  • Authors: Yongxi Zhu, Ziyi Wang, Yichen Wu, Ruite Lu, Liao Chang, Haipeng Li, Ying Wei
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33827
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33827
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33827]] FEAST: Fair and Efficient Adaptive Sampling for Task-Free On-Policy Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33827)
  • Keywords: protect, fair
  • Abstract: Language agents trained from self-generated experience must allocate a limited rollout budget across heterogeneous capabilities. A common strategy is to prioritize capabilities with strong current learning signals, but in on-policy learning these signals are endogenous: past allocation changes the policy and therefore the evidence available for future allocation. This can create a feedback loop in which under-trained capabilities receive increasingly fewer opportunities to improve. We formalize this effect through the Quota-Efficiency Law (QEL), which shows that allocation history can change learning efficiency even under matched capability-specific exposure. Motivated by this, we introduce FEAST, a task-free adaptive sampling framework that separates opportunity allocation from compute allocation. FEAST dynamically discovers capability clusters while preserving their learning histories, maintains continued sampling opportunities across clusters, and reserves a rollout budget for each selected cluster. Within each budget, it progressively concentrates computation on prompts with informative reward variation. Across GURU-18K and SMC, FEAST improves average performance over competitive adaptive sampling baselines under matched rollout budgets, while better preserving learning progress on weaker capabilities. Ablation studies further support the complementary roles of cross-cluster opportunity protection and within-cluster selective allocation. Code will be released upon acceptance.

Title: The Missing Root: Geometric Imagination Without Images in Large Language Models

  • Authors: Loïc Magnan, Tomasz Kozlowski
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33841
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33841

  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: This paper proposes a new paradigm and method for giving large language models spatial understanding. Standard language models operate exclusively over discrete words (secondary representations of things rather than continuous space itself), forcing them to guess geometry verbally. Whereas most multimodal approaches equate spatial grounding with camera images or video frames, spatial structure itself is not a picture: it consists of coordinate frames, solid boundaries, and how objects move and relate to each other in continuous space. Title: The Gradient Difference Induced by Detaching the Denominator in QK Normalization

  • Authors: Yuanqi Zhang, Haocheng Wang, Congping Lin
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33887
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33887
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33887]] The Gradient Difference Induced by Detaching the Denominator in QK Normalization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33887)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: QK normalization divides each query and key by its own RMS norm before the attention scores are computed. Treating this denominator as a constant during backpropagation leaves the forward pass unchanged but alters the parameter gradients. We derive a closed form for the difference between the gradient under this detached rule and the gradient under the standard rule. For a single token, this difference is parallel to the token's own query; it changes only the query norm and has no first-order effect after normalization. The queries, however, are computed by a projection matrix $W_Q$ shared across all tokens. The gradient difference on $W_Q$ therefore equals $W_Q$ right-multiplied by a weighted second moment of the inputs, and it decomposes exactly into three components: a radial component that changes only the norm of $W_Q$, a tangential component that changes the query directions of other tokens, and an upstream component that changes the gradient passed to earlier layers. Under AdamW, the radial component drives $W_Q$ to a larger equilibrium norm and reduces its angular update per step. Paired experiments on a small Transformer language model are consistent with this analysis. Detaching the query denominator consistently raises the test loss, and the gap persists across hardware, evaluation datasets and learning rates. All three components remain non-negligible throughout training, with the tangential component the largest. Norm growth and the smaller angular update appear only on the detached projection matrix, while the other projection matrix is essentially unchanged. With a learnable scale, however, we observe no resolvable loss gap.
