Title: InstructTTS-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Multi-Objective Instruction-Following TTS

  • Authors: Haitao Li, Tian Tan, Yuguang Yang, Guanrou Yang, Wenhao Guan, Chenglin Li, Shan Yang, Xie Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, eess.AS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33937
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33937
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33937]] InstructTTS-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Multi-Objective Instruction-Following TTS(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33937)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Instruction-following text-to-speech (Instruct-TTS) renders a target transcript in the manner described by a free-form natural-language instruction, and a good rendition must succeed on three fronts at once: following the instruction, pronouncing the transcript correctly, and sounding natural. Reinforcement learning has become the standard way to post-train such generative models toward human preferences, and TTS systems increasingly adopt it. When the three objectives are optimized together under a single scalar reward, typically with group relative policy optimization (GRPO), training tends to be unstable: the objectives compete, and the shared update drifts toward whichever one is easiest to improve, producing a seesaw among them. We instead adapt on-policy distillation (OPD), which fuses several single-objective teachers by matching their velocity fields on the student's own rollouts. Realizing this for Instruct-TTS first calls for a sound reward on each axis, which we analyze carefully: a distilled Qwen-Omni proxy for the costly Gemini judge, dialect-free data for the WER reward, and DNSMOS in place of the language-biased UTMOS. We then train three single-objective teachers with GRPO and fuse them with OPD, routing each rollout to the instruction-following or WER teacher by its data domain and letting every rollout additionally follow the MOS teacher for quality. This lets instruction following and intelligibility improve together without a trade-off, but the quality target conflicts with instruction following, so following it additively reintroduces the seesaw. We therefore propose conflict-aware velocity fusion, which projects the quality target off the direction that opposes the primary objective before fusing, and show that it markedly reduces the instruction-following/quality trade-off. The fused model improves over scalar-reward GRPO on all three objectives at once.

Title: CondUQ: Calibrated Conditional Uncertainty Quantification Using Distribution Matching

  • Authors: Shizhuang Wang, Tianhao Wang, Xingqun Zhan
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33939
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33939
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33939]] CondUQ: Calibrated Conditional Uncertainty Quantification Using Distribution Matching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33939)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Many applications, especially reliability- and safety-critical ones, require accurate uncertainty quantification for the outputs of model- or learning-based measurement, estimation, and prediction systems. The errors of such systems often vary with factors such as input quality and operating conditions, and thus cannot be accurately described by a single Gaussian model. Although Gaussian mixture models and calibrated quantiles can approximate such error distributions, their unconditional forms do not capture how the error behavior depends on these factors. Hence, we propose condUQ, an approach that conditions error models on observable factors for enabling accurate and reliable uncertainty quantification. condUQ is built on top of an existing system without modifying it and applies even when the system is a black box. First, condUQ maps the observable factors to a latent quality index (QI) such that errors sharing the same QI follow a common, approximately Gaussian conditional distribution. The QI predictor is trained end-to-end with a distribution-matching objective, without requiring quality labels. Second, for each QI, a Gaussian error model is then fitted and calibrated on a large held-out dataset. condUQ is demonstrated on three examples: (a) GPS multipath errors conditioned on satellite elevation and signal strength, (b) relative attitude errors of a transformer-based visual relative pose regression model conditioned on its internal features of the image pairs, and (c) prediction errors of a world model conditioned on the input observations and actions. In all three examples, condUQ substantially reduces the discrepancy between the modeled and empirical error distributions compared with a single global model. Moreover, the learned QIs reveal how the underlying factors affect the error characteristics in a manner consistent with physical principles.

  • Abstract: Looped multi-view reconstruction refines scene features by repeatedly applying a shared transformer block. In Déjà View, approximation errors are attenuated more strongly than matched random perturbations under a scale-adjusted state-error measure, and a local Jacobian decomposition separates the contribution of channel gating from the response to spatial error structure. Across five reconstruction benchmarks, the balanced configuration runs $1.63\times$ faster than the official 16-step Déjà View model with a 1.86-point reduction in average pose AUC@$3^\circ$. Title: DuoMI: Dual Mutual Information Regularization for Expert Reuse in Looped Transformers

  • Authors: Xinlai Kang, Zhenhe Wu, Haoyu Wang, Weihua He, Yaping Jin, Jie Hu, Hanting Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33952
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.33952
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.33952]] DuoMI: Dual Mutual Information Regularization for Expert Reuse in Looped Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33952)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: We introduce Dual Mutual Information Regularization (DuoMI), a method for regulating expert routing across rounds in looped mixture-of-experts (MoE) Transformers. When two rounds of computation share an expert pool, their routers may favor different expert subsets, limiting the reuse of individual expert parameters across rounds. Even when aggregate expert usage is identical across rounds, we would still prefer each token to retain some experts while switching to others. DuoMI addresses both aspects of routing: it uses round-level marginal mutual information to limit the dependence of aggregate expert usage on the round, and token-level conditional mutual information to penalize average cross-round routing changes that fall outside a target interval. During training, DuoMI computes differentiable penalties from soft routing distributions over all experts. It adds no model parameters and leaves sparse Top-$k$ routing strategy unchanged at inference. On a looped OLMoE backbone in which paired adjacent layers share experts, DuoMI improves the average score across $15$ downstream tasks from $50.08\%$ to $51.82\%$, a gain of $1.74$ percentage points over the looped baseline.

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Online forward matching aligns flow-based generative models by repeatedly generating, rewarding, and fitting model-produced endpoints. However, this behavior creates an on-policy feedback loop: as high-reward modes become increasingly favored, alternative modes become less likely to appear in subsequent training pools, progressively reducing visual diversity. We introduce RefMix, a reference-assisted endpoint proposal strategy that addresses this limitation by injecting alternative candidates into the training pool while retaining the original task reward and forward matching objective. WSP never collapses and is deterministic, but it needs one encoder pass per template (17 here). We report all eight recipe versions, every decision that saw test results, and 27 ideas that did not help.

We introduce MolGPAO (Molecular Generative Pre-trained Autoregressive Octree), a standalone 3D octree architecture and single-output speculative tree-decoding engine using zero external surrogates, zero reinforcement learning, and zero inference-time MLIP relaxation. Across GEOM-Drugs (0.49 GPU-h), disjoint 50k/50k QM9 (0.43 GPU-h), and PBE0/def2-TZVP-verified OE62 (0.56 GPU-h), MolGPAO lifts drug-scale valid-connected yield 6.78x (12.7% to 86.2%, 99.83% atom stability), runs 7.3x faster (or 1.65x faster at 2.0x yield) than 100-step FlowMol at $N=100$, beats 156 GPU-h PropMolFlow on QM9 HOMO (-12.7%) and dipole (-21.0%) at 362x lower compute, and doubles live DFT hit rate on OE62 (33.3% vs. 16.7%) at 92.8x lower compute.

  • Abstract: Organisations deploying large language models in Ukrainian-language information systems need to know whether a model produces normative Ukrainian.   • Abstract: Unsupervised cross-modal homography estimation is challenging because appearance differences obscure shared geometric structures.   • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34032
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34032

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Deep-learning-generated media are increasingly passed off as real, driving an arms race between image generators and deepfake detectors. Counterfactual examples, which show the minimal change that flips a detector's decision, reveal what evidence drives a verdict, helping analysts decide whether to trust it. This work introduces Counter-DECEIVR (DEtector-guided reCtifiEd flow InVeRsion for counterfactuals), a novel training-free framework that generates counterfactual examples. It also leverages the detector's explanation maps to concentrate editing to salient regions, turning low-level feature importance into localised counterfactual edits. A single parameter controls the strength of the edits. Across two deepfake detectors and two widely used forensics datasets, Counter-DECEIVR outperforms prior counterfactual generation methods, including training-based ones. Code will be made available upon acceptance.

Title: Hijacking 3D Generation: Targeted Adversarial Attacks against Image-to-3D Models via Prefix Trajectory Alignment

  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI

  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Online services that deploy Image-to-3D models enable users to generate high-quality 3D assets from a single image. To prevent such content, these services typically use policy-restricted inspectors to examine uploaded images before generation. Since the inspector examines only the visible content of the input, a visually benign image can still induce the generation of policy-restricted 3D geometry. However, turning this vulnerability into an effective attack is non-trivial in the black-box setting: global backpropagation through the long DiT generation trajectory is difficult to fit, while different image preprocessing pipelines across services can substantially reduce transferability. In this paper, we present PTA3D, a targeted adversarial attack against Image-to-3D models that addresses these challenges through Prefix Trajectory Alignment (PTA) and Preprocessing Aware Optimization (PAO). PTA progressively aligns the prefix DiT outputs of the adversarial trajectory with those of the target trajectory, where the core geometric structure is formed, allowing the final geometry to be controlled without optimizing through the complete generation trajectory. PAO exposes adversarial optimization to diverse image transformations rather than a single preprocessing pipeline, reducing sensitivity to the mismatch between surrogate and target services. We establish a prefix-to-output bound and a preprocessing-aware transfer bound to theoretically characterize the effectiveness of the two designs. Experiments on three open-source Image-to-3D models and three online services show that PTA3D achieves 41.5%-88.5% ASR on online services and 90.8%-94.6% ASR in white box settings, substantially outperforming existing attacks.

Title: Projector Subspace Optimization for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning

  • Authors: Hao Yu, Quan Cheng, Lijun Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34078
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34078
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34078]] Projector Subspace Optimization for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34078)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance through instruction tuning, yet adapting them to sequential tasks requires Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT). Existing replay-free methods expand prompts or routed LoRA experts across multi-layer decoders, incurring heavy parameter and routing overhead while shifting internal feature distributions. Instead, we identify the final linear layer of the vision-language projector as a high-capacity, coordinate-stationary bridge, where sequential updates easily induce interface drift by displacing historical visual tokens. While low-rank subspace adaptation offers a compact remedy, selecting directions solely from historical statistics or task gradients sacrifices plasticity or stability, and unconstrained multi-step updates still accumulate large drift. To resolve both issues, we propose Projector Refinement through Optimized Update Directions (PROUD), a continual subspace optimization framework for the terminal projector layer. At task onset, PROUD combines retained input statistics with initial gradients to analytically extract a metric-orthonormal subspace maximizing first-order task gain under a drift budget. During training, it regularizes updates within this fixed subspace via an exact Frobenius penalty before losslessly merging them with zero inference or routing overhead. Experiments on UCIT and CoIN across MLLM backbones show that PROUD outperforms recent replay-free baselines in continual accuracy while curbing forgetting with a fraction of trainable parameters.

  • Abstract: Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) allows a wireless system to use the same waveform for communication and environment sensing. Across four classification tasks, MUSE produces reliable uncertainty estimates that enable selective prediction, improving accuracy by abstaining on the least confident 20% of predictions. We further improve the reliability of the model's confidence estimates using temperature scaling and conformal prediction. Finally, MUSE's uncertainty signal remains reliable even under cross-user deployment, assigning higher uncertainty to wrong predictions than to correct ones 78% of the time, and recovering 9.2 percentage points of accuracy through selective prediction, with no additional retraining on the unseen user.

Title: Out of Sight, Not Out of Clues: Can Video LLMs Guess from Partial Evidence?

  • Authors: Luoxi Zhang, Jiawei Liu, Yanlin Luo, Peiyang He, Junlin Zhu, Chun To Choi, Yang Xiong, Jiang Wu, Xingyu Zeng, Min Yang, Shiwen Ni

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: In real-world applications like egocentric recording, visual accessibility, and surveillance review, visual evidence for Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) is often only partially captured. Yet existing benchmarks enforce a binary answerable-or-unanswerable split, leaving partial evidence unmeasured and obscuring whether failures arise from missing evidence or misjudging sufficiency. Title: FoundSurface: Feedforward Ray-Based 3D Scene Surface Reconstruction from Unposed Images

  • Abstract: Accurate 3D surface reconstruction from unposed images remains a fundamental challenge in computer vision. Title: SOC-Net: Second-Order-Cone-Constrained Neural Networks

  • Authors: Seungseok Oh, Youngjae Min, Navid Azizan, Namhoon Cho
  • Subjects: cs.LG, eess.SY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34136
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34136
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34136]] SOC-Net: Second-Order-Cone-Constrained Neural Networks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34136)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: In many applications, neural networks must learn input-output maps that respect prior knowledge such as physical laws, safety requirements, and operating limits. These requirements constrain which outputs are admissible for a given input and often admit second-order cone (SOC) representations, including norm bounds and robust linear inequalities. Examples in control include friction cones, thrust bounds, and input-robust control barrier function (CBF) conditions. However, existing approaches to enforcing general input-dependent SOC constraints require a conic solver, an iterative scheme whose feasibility depends on convergence, or an interior feasible point for each input. We propose SOC-Net, a neural network architecture that enforces input-dependent SOC constraints by construction for every input, even outside the training distribution. SOC-Net projects an affine lifting of the backbone output onto the reachable slice of a fixed product of Lorentz cones and maps the correction back to the output space. For separable constraints, this projection splits into one projection per cone, and each is available in closed form or reduces to a single scalar equation, regardless of the cone dimension. We prove that SOC-Net retains the expressive power of the backbone network under regularity conditions and accommodates end-to-end training by standard backpropagation with an explicit Jacobian. Experiments on contact force allocation with friction constraints and 3-D obstacle avoidance with an input-robust CBF demonstrate that SOC-Net satisfies the constraints at every evaluated input, with lower evaluation latency than the tested solver-based and iterative baselines.

  • Abstract: Recent 3D generative models can produce high-quality meshes with complex structures and fine geometric details, with broad applications in games, film, and digital design. Yet existing benchmarks mostly report end-to-end success rates, which hide whether a failure stems from visual perception, rule application, or multi-step planning. Title: Familiar Words, Unfamiliar Minds: Conventionalization without Calibration in the Vocabulary of Mind

  • Authors: Matthieu Queloz
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34179
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34179
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34179]] Familiar Words, Unfamiliar Minds: Conventionalization without Calibration in the Vocabulary of Mind(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34179)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Speakers describe new technologies by stretching old words, letting experience of the new referents sort out which inferences carry over. I argue that stretching the vocabulary of mind to language models pulls apart two processes that theories of metaphor and linguistic change tend to conflate: conventionalization, whereby a stretched word ceases to be marked as such, and calibration, whereby the inferences it invites are fitted to what its new referents bear out. As a gene variant can reach fixation without being an adaptation, a word can become conventional without being calibrated. A corpus study of "hallucinate" in 8,568 abstracts and 31,866 forum comments shows conventionalization running most of its course within three years. Calibration need not keep pace: these models are organized so unlike human minds that it is hard to tell when mental words apply and what they imply. Even warranted attributions can mislead: mechanistic analysis shows that a transformer trained to navigate Manhattan has and uses a faithful street map, yet loses track of its position on long, diverted journeys. Nor is the gap likely to close by itself. The usual routes to calibration (direct experience, designers' knowledge, experiment) presuppose a referent that is accessible, stable, and independent of the discourse about it. But language models are opaque, fast-changing, and looped into the discourse: they are trained on past uses of mental vocabulary and on the experimental literature about them, and join the discourse themselves. Calibration must therefore be organized, through a reciprocal, not merely deferential, division of linguistic labour. This yields a norm of targeted qualification (flag the specific inferences an attribution invites without support), a reply to the worry that meaning change eludes deliberate control, and three testable predictions about when qualifications calibrate expectations.

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Change point detection (CPD) methods aim to identify abrupt changes in the properties of time series, which is a key problem in numerous fields. A major class of CPD approaches relies on computing a CPD score, which is either a discrepancy or divergence between two subsequent samples, called windows. A fundamental limitation of window-based CPD is the 'sample size bottleneck.' To ensure temporal precision, windows must be small; however, small samples provide insufficient statistical evidence for the classifiers and deep-learning models currently used to compute CPD scores. This leads to high variance in the scores and frequent mispredictions. Our study tackles this issue by exploring generative models for window augmentation, effectively expanding available data to improve CPD quality. Experiments on synthetic and real data demonstrate that the proposed approach significantly enhances CPD. Specifically, we show that this approach allows simple classifiers to outperform complex deep-learning baselines by stabilizing the CPD score distribution.

Title: Evaluating Post-Quantum and Hybrid Signatures for Blockchains: Performance, Storage and Ledger Growth

  • Authors: Anthony Mikhaiel, Essam Ghadafi
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34229
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34229

  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Advances in quantum computing pose a long-term threat to blockchain systems that rely on classical digital signature schemes such as ECDSA. Migrating these systems to post-quantum cryptography introduces practical costs beyond cryptographic security, including larger transactions, higher computational costs and faster ledger growth. We present an empirical evaluation of 13 signature configurations in a common simulated blockchain framework: ECDSA, ML-DSA-44/65/87, Falcon-512/1024, SLH-DSA-SHA2-128s, and six hybrids combining ECDSA with each post-quantum scheme. Storage overheads, however, are substantial. Hybrid configurations introduce relatively modest additional storage overhead compared with their standalone post-quantum counterparts, while ECDSA+Falcon-512 approximately doubles the verification cost of standalone Falcon-512. Existing transaction- and script-size limits also present obstacles to straightforward migration in Bitcoin and Solana. Overall, our findings suggest that storage overhead may pose a greater challenge than signature verification costs when migrating blockchain systems to post-quantum cryptography.

Title: TORE: Token-Wise Taylor Order Selection via Decomposed Residual Error Estimation for Diffusion Acceleration

  • Authors: Lei Tang, Baoquan Zhang, Yakun Zhang, Yunming Ye
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34261
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34261
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34261]] TORE: Token-Wise Taylor Order Selection via Decomposed Residual Error Estimation for Diffusion Acceleration(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34261)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion models excel at image and video generation, but iterative denoising incurs substantial inference latency. Recent token-adaptive acceleration methods select Taylor prediction orders for the full-model residual using shallow-layer proxy scores. However, candidate orders are evaluated on these proxy features but used to predict the full-model residual, creating a proxy--target mismatch: the proxy-preferred order may not minimize residual prediction error. To address this mismatch, we propose \textbf{TORE} (\textbf{T}aylor \textbf{O}rder selection via \textbf{R}esidual \textbf{E}rror estimation), which selects token-wise Taylor orders based on estimated prediction errors for the full-model residual. Yet direct error evaluation requires future ground-truth residuals, which are unavailable during order selection. TORE therefore estimates prediction error by analyzing how errors arise in Taylor extrapolation. Specifically, it decomposes prediction error into truncation error and finite-difference derivative error, then estimates both contributions from cached residuals to preselect token-wise orders for upcoming skip steps. Extensive experiments show that TORE achieves $4.31\times$ speedup on FLUX.1-dev and $4.18\times$ on Wan2.1 while maintaining high generation quality and fidelity.

  • Authors: Manit Baser, Vyoman Jain, Rachel Yeo Hui Min, Xianglin Yang, Meenatchi Sundaram Muthu Selva Annamalai, Dinil Mon Divakaran

  • Abstract: As large language models increasingly collaborate in heterogeneous multi-agent systems, latent communication has emerged as an efficient alternative to exchanging natural-language messages. Title: RAP: Radial-Angular Random Projection for Hyperbolic Representations

  • Authors: Jiahong Liu, Wenhao Yu, Yifan Li, Yanyu Chen, Rex Ying, Menglin Yang, Irwin King
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34298
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34298
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34298]] RAP: Radial-Angular Random Projection for Hyperbolic Representations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34298)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Random projection is a basic compression primitive for Euclidean representation learning, with the Johnson--Lindenstrauss lemma giving a target dimension $k=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ independent of the ambient dimension. As hyperbolic representations gain increasing attention in modern Transformers, vision-language models, and large language models, the same need for oblivious dimensionality reduction arises, but no analogous primitive yet exists for hyperbolic representations. We study this gap. Two natural attempts fail in dual ways: a tangent-space scheme gives a clean local guarantee but is chart-bound and pays a curvature penalty, while a fully hyperbolic projection in the hyperboloid model is geometrically native but mixes radial and angular error before feeding them into the conditioning of $\operatorname{arcosh}$. We propose radial-angular random projection (RAP), which preserves the radial coordinate exactly and applies a Euclidean random projection only to the angular direction, lifting the result back to the target hyperboloid. RAP is hyperbolic-to-hyperbolic by construction, costs one Gaussian matrix-vector product, and reduces hyperbolic compression to a normalized angular JL question on $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$. We prove a finite-set RAP guarantee with explicit $\sinh(\sqrt{c}\,r_x)\sinh(\sqrt{c}\,r_y)$ conditioning, complemented by a local tangent-space JL theorem and an arcosh-conditioning lemma for the fully hyperbolic comparator. Across an origin-centered broad-radius point cloud, a balanced tree, a clustered Gaussian, and the WordNet noun hierarchy, RAP attains the lowest mean relative distortion in every $(n,k)$ cell, reducing distortion over the strongest hyperbolic baseline by approximately $48\%$--$49\%$ on broad-radius data. RAP also scales to million-point datasets and improves retrieval on vision--language representations over hyperbolic projection baselines.

Title: Attacking Hard-Label Large Vision-Language Models with Model-Sensitive Adversarial Patch Designs

  • Authors: Nian Ai, Guangke Chen, Xiaowen Cai, Zhongliang Guo, Daizong Liu, Pan Zhou, Ognjen Arandjelović
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34467
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34467
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34467]] Attacking Hard-Label Large Vision-Language Models with Model-Sensitive Adversarial Patch Designs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34467)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities in handling multi-modal downstream tasks, gaining increasing popularity. However, recent studies show that LVLMs are susceptible to both intentional and inadvertent attacks. Existing attackers ideally optimize adversarial perturbations with backpropagated gradients from LVLMs, thus limiting their scalability in practical scenarios as real-world LVLM applications will not provide any LVLM's gradient or details. Motivated by this research gap and counter-practical phenomenon, we propose a novel hard-label attack method for LVLMs, named HardPatch, to generate visual adversarial patches by solely querying the model. Our method provides deeper insights into how to investigate the vulnerability of LVLMs in local visual regions and generate corresponding adversarial substitution under the practical yet challenging hard-label setting. Specifically, we first split each image into uniform patches and mask each of them to individually assess their sensitivity to the LVLM model. Then, according to the descending order of sensitive scores, we iteratively select the most vulnerable patch to initialize noise and estimate gradients with further additive random noises for optimization. In this manner, multiple patches are perturbed until the altered image satisfies the adversarial condition. Extensive LVLM models and datasets are evaluated to demonstrate the adversarial nature of HardPatch. Our empirical observations suggest that with appropriate patch substitution and optimization, HardPatch can craft effective adversarial images to attack hard-label LVLMs.

Title: Fuel as Identity: Authenticating Inter-Satellite Laser Links after Total Key Compromise

  • Authors: Leon Birkel, Mohammad Hamad, Sebastian Steinhorst
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34470
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34470
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34470]] Fuel as Identity: Authenticating Inter-Satellite Laser Links after Total Key Compromise(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34470)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Optical inter-satellite links form the backbone of entire constellations in low Earth orbit, yet their security rests on keys alone. When an attacker obtains every key of a satellite, no secret is left to authenticate it again. Existing fixes fail, since ground rekeying encrypts new keys under the leaked ones, and post-compromise protocols assume an authenticated start. As a remedy, the satellite's orbit can take over as the trust anchor. The attacker then needs an impostor satellite that blocks and retransmits the beam, a chord beneath the curved orbit. Hence, the blocking point lies off free fall and costs the impostor continuous thrust. We formalize this position-gated adversary and prove, for satellites on a shared circular orbit, a tight lower bound on the propellant cost in delta-v of a point-mass craft that keeps blocking, however it moves. We present OrbitLock, a history-chained protocol that combines this position gate with ML-KEM. Each link authenticates on its own, and we sketch how combining links forces an impostor onto every link. Once the chain outlasts an impostor's propellant, every key back to the first is authenticated retroactively. A satellite thus needs no crosslink key before launch, so none can leak in the supply chain. The same mechanism heals the link after total key compromise, even without compromise awareness. A passive attacker is shut out at the next round, whereas an active one splits the chains and is exposed once its propellant runs out. The chain can then heal after an operator reset. As a result, every Starlink satellite has a partner on whose link a CubeSat-class impostor runs out of propellant in about two days. This holds against one rigid craft per link running the best attack we know, if each terminal enforces a 1 km exclusion zone, while the protocol only uses under 0.02% of the link.

Title: Who Is Ego? Scaling Cross-View Camera Wearer Identification and Segmentation beyond Humans

  • Authors: Yifan Jin, Zheng Zhang, Tianwen Qian, Dong Wang, Bo Zhao, Yanwei Fu, Yuqian Fu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.RO

  • Abstract: A first-person (ego) image reveals what a camera sees, but not who or what carries it. Title: Where Does the Asymmetry Enter? Staging a Fairness Audit Across a Fused Multimodal Moderation Pipeline

  • Authors: Kardelen Subasi, Jarosław A. Chudziak
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CY, cs.MM
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34491
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34491
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34491]] Where Does the Asymmetry Enter? Staging a Fairness Audit Across a Fused Multimodal Moderation Pipeline(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34491)
  • Keywords: fair, segmentation
  • Abstract: Almost every short-video upload now passes through a classifier that fuses video, speech, and on-screen text into one decision about whether it may be seen. It moderates every language community on the platform at once, and fairness across them is judged, if at all, from one accuracy score. That score is compatible with error falling almost entirely on one language group. An audit that goes looking reads the model's decisions, the last stage of a long pipeline and the one whose test has the fewest items. We ask where in a fused pipeline a subgroup asymmetry can be detected at all: in what the classifier may read, in what its branches encode, or in what it decides. We answer this with a post-hoc audit of a published TikTok classifier, holding all three stages to one significance standard using only its logged decisions, labels, and pre-fusion features, with source language as the subgroup axis. The asymmetry is significant at the stage deciding what the model may read, and again in what the text branch encodes. At the earlier stage, a language-blind cap admits less of a Vietnamese clip than an English one, for reasons of segmentation, not verbosity. The asymmetry is undetectable at the decision, where the smallest subgroup leaves twelve items to test. An audit reading only decisions looks where its evidence is thinnest; the same test staged upstream recovers a disparity the decision-level view returns as noise.

Title: Know the Design, Pick the Attack: Sentence-Keyed Watermarks for LLMs

  • Authors: Jon Carmona, Jon Irureta, Jon Imaz, Aizea Lojo, Juan Martínez-Romo, Lourdes Araujo
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34505
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34505
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34505]] Know the Design, Pick the Attack: Sentence-Keyed Watermarks for LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34505)
  • Keywords: attack, watermark
  • Abstract: A watermark lets the provider of a language model recognise the text its model wrote, which matters more as generated text becomes hard to tell from human writing: provenance underpins academic integrity, the fight against misinformation and new regulation. Providers have started to watermark the text of their models, and to explain how their watermarks work. Explaining a watermark, however, also tells an attacker how to break it, and there are two ways to do so: reword the text to erase the mark, or learn the mark and copy it onto text the model never wrote. Each attack has been studied on its own. We study an attacker that reads the published design to choose between them: it erases the watermarks that are hard to learn and forges the ones that survive rewording. Against it, every existing watermark we compare fails. We propose KeyedLex, which reads its secret key from the grammar of the previous sentence, which rewording changes little, and whose detector does not need to recover the key exactly. It outperforms every watermark we compare on both models we test, losing about half of its signal where the best alternative loses two thirds, at no extra cost in text quality over the best-known watermarks.

  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: It is commonly accepted that high-level concepts are represented linearly in the representation space of large neural networks. To find them, the classical approach is to train a linear classifier on the representations, but it needs a large amount of labels and only recovers what can be read from the representations, not what the model actually uses. In this paper we start from the following insight: The LLM Decoder of an LVLM is also a classifier whose classes are the possible next tokens. So we use its first-order Taylor expansion as a linear classifier. Hence, we introduce Decoder-as-a-Probe (DaP), which significantly outperforms classical probing and steering baselines especially when training data is sparse. (1) It verbalizes what an LVLM perceives in a given image. (2) It can surgically change the perception of the LVLM about specific attributes (e.g. change only the color, the clothes worn or the action done). Due to its simplicity and data efficiency, we believe DaP can serve as a plug-and-play tool for LVLM research, such as interpretability, hallucination mitigation or instruction tuning.

Title: PURA: Video Token Pruning via Unified Spatiotemporal Redundancy Assessment

  • Authors: Yuyao Sun, Hongli Yuan, Shuang Li, Tao Deng, Deqing Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34521
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34521
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34521]] PURA: Video Token Pruning via Unified Spatiotemporal Redundancy Assessment(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34521)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Video large language models incur substantial inference costs from long visual token sequences. Existing video token compression methods mitigate these costs by exploiting spatial redundancy within frames and temporal redundancy across frames. However, many approaches impose predefined processing orders or fixed allocation ratios for spatial and temporal compression, limiting their ability to adapt to the varying balance of spatiotemporal redundancy across videos. To address this limitation, we propose PURA, a training-free method that prunes visual tokens through unified spatiotemporal redundancy assessment. For each token, PURA constructs spatial and temporal reference candidates and selects the reference with the lowest representation error across the two dimensions. The representation error is combined with token importance to determine each token's removal cost. To avoid the time overhead of removing the lowest-cost token one by one, we introduce Coordinated Pruning, which iteratively groups mutually compatible low-cost removals into batches for simultaneous execution and updates the pruning state after each batch until the target budget is reached. Extensive experiments on three video large language models and five video understanding benchmarks demonstrate that PURA achieves a favorable trade-off between task performance and inference efficiency.

Title: Score-based Black-box Attack via Adversarial Condition Guided Diffusion Model

  • Authors: Yuhang Chen, Chih-Hong Cheng, Kurt Debattista, Siddartha Khastgir, Xingyu Zhao

  • Abstract: Generative models enable unrestricted adversarial attacks that aim to fool classifiers while preserving the natural appearance and semantics of the original image. However, existing diffusion-based black-box attacks largely rely on transferability from surrogates, making their effectiveness sensitive to differences between surrogate and victim. Improving transferability can also distort semantic content and degrade image quality. Moreover, retaining the original prompt to preserve fidelity leaves the diffusion model's conditional prior underused for adversarial guidance. We address these limitations by adapting score-based black-box attacks to reverse diffusion, using the victim's output probabilities to guide latent updates without accessing its gradients. This achieves comparable attack effectiveness with better image quality than runner-up guidance. Experiments on the ImageNet-Compatible dataset show that our framework outperforms transfer-based baselines in both attack effectiveness and image quality.

Title: Tune for Retention, Not Just Gain: SFT Forgets Far Less than You Think

  • Authors: Nirmit Joshi, Nathan Srebro, Eran Malach
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34579
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34579
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34579]] Tune for Retention, Not Just Gain: SFT Forgets Far Less than You Think(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34579)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting, the drastic loss of previously acquired capabilities when learning new ones, is a central challenge in language model fine-tuning. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is widely believed to be prone to such forgetting. Recent works have attributed this to the off-policy nature of SFT, arguing that on-policy learning is key to continual learning. In this paper, we challenge this premise. We show that while maximizing new-task performance can induce catastrophic forgetting, alternative hyperparameter choices (lower learning rate and batch size) can yield similar gains with little to no forgetting. With hyperparameters selected jointly for gain and retention, vanilla SFT retains prior capabilities far better than previously suggested and can be competitive with Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT), an on-policy approach designed to mitigate forgetting. While SDFT can be more robust to hyperparameter choices, it also requires a retention-aware criterion to identify hyperparameter configurations that avoid catastrophic forgetting. Our results challenge the view that on-policy training is what enables learning with retention and raise broader questions about what drives catastrophic forgetting during fine-tuning.

  • Abstract: The severe susceptibility of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to hallucinations poses a critical threat to their reliability, making a rigorous evaluation framework indispensable. Driven by these innovative structural designs, SEAM establishes an automated and self-evolving evaluation paradigm. Experiments across four target MLLMs and ten hallucination types demonstrate that SEAM substantially outperforms representative static benchmarks, achieving an 89.2\% relative improvement in mean valid hallucination discovery rate across 12 matched comparisons.

Title: Beyond Pose Control: Anatomically Consistent Ultrasound Video Generation

  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Camera-controlled video generation anticipates how a scene appears from new viewpoints by relying on a prior learned implicitly from large-scale data. Ultrasound poses the same problem, with the probe pose as the viewpoint, but under opposite conditions: anatomy offers an explicit prior that every sonographer relies on, whereas tracked scans are too scarce to learn it implicitly. As a result, pose control alone is not enough, and pose-conditioned ultrasound generators produce implausible anatomy in unscanned regions and inconsistent anatomy on revisits. We go beyond pose control and give the generator the two kinds of knowledge that make up a sonographer's mental model. An anatomical prior, built from a population of subjects, guides generation where nothing has been scanned, and is refined online by every generated chunk towards the scanned subject. A 3D latent memory, designed around the cross-sectional physics of ultrasound, compounds the generated slices into a volume of the patient and reslices it at new poses, keeping revisited anatomy consistent. The fine-grained memory enters the generator at the input, while the coarse anatomy prior guides the layout across transformer blocks. Experiments on thyroid, kidney, and lumbar spine show improved visual fidelity, pose following, and anatomical consistency over pose-conditioned and memory-augmented baselines.

Title: Distractor-Robust Reinforcement Learning via Variational Bisimulation

  • Authors: Benjamin Freed, Vivaan Singh, Dean Zadok, Roberto Calandra, Jeff Schneider, Howie Choset
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34641
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34641
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34641]] Distractor-Robust Reinforcement Learning via Variational Bisimulation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34641)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) promises data efficiency and generalization, but typical reconstruction-based objectives encourage models to waste representational capacity on task-irrelevant distractors. We introduce VIBES (Variational Inference for Bisimulation-based Encoded States), a new objective that replaces pixel reconstruction with an adversarial term, which enforces that latent states suffice to predict both rewards and the next latent state. We show that this objective measures how far the encoder is from reward and dynamics sufficiency, and that whenever exact sufficiency is attainable, its global optima induce bisimulation relations on histories. Unlike prior approaches, it does not rely on image-specific augmentations and applies equally well to high-dimensional vector-state tasks, demonstrated on a 100-link swimmer robot. Finally, latent-space analyses (UMAP embeddings and nearest-neighbor probes) confirm that the learned representations are sensitive to task-relevant structure while invariant to distractors.

Title: Counting Beyond Frames: Codec Motion Tells Frozen Diffusion VLMs How Many

  • Authors: Zhuoqin Li, Jiayu Ding, Ge Li
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34657
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34657
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34657]] Counting Beyond Frames: Codec Motion Tells Frozen Diffusion VLMs How Many(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34657)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Counting how many times something happens is among the simplest questions about a video and among the hardest for video-language models, autoregressive and diffusion alike. The difficulty lies between the frames: a model samples only a few of them, while each repetition unfolds as movement in between. Video codecs already record this movement densely as block displacements, and codec-based video models use it to shape their visual input. We are the first to show that this motion provides direct evidence for the count: a candidate count is a testable motion hypothesis, and unwinding the cycles it implies measures how strongly the observed directions support it. We present CoDiCount, a unified training-free framework that turns this test into a count potential and applies it while a frozen diffusion vision-language model (dVLM) denoises its answer. At every denoising step, structured count messages map each option letter, or each grammar-valid edit of an open integer, to the count it expresses and reweight the model's own probabilities within that set. Consistent gains over the official decoders on MotionBench, TOMATO, and SVCBench demonstrate the broad generalization of CoDiCount across diffusion models, datasets, and diverse counting tasks. With CoDiCount, a 7B dVLM outperforms GPT-5.1 and Gemini-2.5-Pro on MotionBench, surpasses GPT-4o and much larger open models on TOMATO, and matches GPT-5.4 on SVCBench.

Title: BridgeHammer: DNN Backdoor Attack via CPU-iGPU Shared-memory Rowhammer

  • Authors: Yufei Wang, Saion K. Roy, Yunsi Fei
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34684
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34684
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34684]] BridgeHammer: DNN Backdoor Attack via CPU-iGPU Shared-memory Rowhammer(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34684)
  • Keywords: protect, attack
  • Abstract: Heterogeneous systems-on-chips (SoCs) share physical memory between CPUs and integrated GPUs (iGPUs), creating a path for CPU-side memory corruption to compromise the integrity of deep learning models used for iGPU inference. Implanting a backdoor that causes targeted misclassifications on trigger-bearing inputs while retaining accuracy on normal inputs requires carefully selected weight changes. However, realizing these changes presents two challenges: where to induce faults, since vulnerable DRAM locations may not contain the targeted weights, and when to intervene, since memory placement and fault injection must align with different stages of the model deployment lifecycle that involve two distinct computation domains. We present BridgeHammer, a Rowhammer-based backdoor attack that addresses these challenges by coordinating targeted weight placement before zero-copy binding for the iGPU and fault injection before inference. The iGPU then uses the corrupted model for subsequent inferences. We evaluate BridgeHammer on a heterogeneous Intel CPU-iGPU platform using OpenVINO, targeting ResNet-20, VGG-16, and ResNet-50 at both INT8 and FP32 precision. Across all six configurations, BridgeHammer achieves an 88.9% attack success rate (ASR) and 86.3% clean accuracy on average, with reliable target-page placement and bit-flip reproduction. These results demonstrate a new integrity vulnerability on heterogeneous CPU-iGPU SoCs, motivating cross-domain memory integrity protections.

Title: Robust Recourse under Causal Graph Ambiguity

  • Authors: Muyan Anna Li, Juliette Sinnott, Mohammad Kohandel, Amir-Hossein Karimi
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34723
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34723
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34723]] Robust Recourse under Causal Graph Ambiguity(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34723)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Causal algorithmic recourse recommends actions to reverse unfavorable decisions by modeling how interventions change an individual's features. Existing methods typically assume one causal graph that describes the system and account for uncertainty in the causal mechanisms. However, there usually exist several graphs that fit the same data but can predict different effects for the same action. Certain actions may achieve recourse across multiple graphs, but as the set of potential graphs grows, a single best action may become infeasible. We propose Worst-Case Recourse and Graph-Weighted Recourse to find a low-cost action that succeeds across all candidate graphs or a specified fraction of graph weight, respectively. Assuming a set of plausible graphs, for each graph we fit Gaussian process structural equations and assess success using the posterior mean--standard-deviation criterion. We use gradient-based search to determine minimal cost actions and check the validity of the actions on fresh posterior samples. In synthetic and semi-synthetic benchmarks, relaxing robustness, the requirement that an action succeed under every candidate graph, can increase the number of valid recommendations for recourse and lower costs of actions for both methods. In the 20-node GP benchmark, relaxation increases the number of factual instances offered recourse (coverage) from 341 to 436 out of 1,000, while the number of true successes, actions that flip the decision under the generating model, falls from 251 to 184, illustrating the coverage--validity trade-off. Accounting for graph ambiguity can help decision-makers recommend actions that remain effective even when the causal mechanisms of the system are not fully known.

Title: Hierarchical Graph Metanetworks

  • Authors: Ioannis Kalogeropoulos, Giorgos Bouritsas, Yannis Panagakis
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34726
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34726

  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: The emerging field of weight space learning has demonstrated the potential to enable metanetworks, i.e. models that process neural network (NN) data, to support several steps of the machine learning lifecycle, from model optimisation to evaluation and adaptation. However, this potential has yet to materialise beyond small-scale MLPs or CNNs: most current models, including message-passing based graph metanetworks (GMNs), operate exclusively at the finest level of granularity, processing every neuron and weight of the input model. Their cost therefore scales with the number of parameters. Yet NNs are inherently organised hierarchically, with neurons forming layers and layers forming blocks and modules. Additionally, several of their properties, such as Lipschitz upper bounds and generalisation measures, are compositions of layer- and block-level quantities. To incorporate this inductive bias, we introduce Hierarchical Graph Metanetworks (H-GMNs), which leverage the input architecture's hierarchy to coarsen and refine the graph using permutation-symmetry-preserving pooling and un-pooling layers. Further, we extend H-GMNs to Transformers by introducing a graph construction that preserves permutation symmetries and enables multi-level pooling. Our evaluation includes new ResNet-18, ViT and varying ViT model zoos of up to 86M parameters, on tasks spanning model evaluation (an analytic Lipschitz bound proxy, generalisation and robustness prediction), testing (backdoor detection) and editing (pruning). Experimental results show that, compared to non-equivariant alternatives and flat GMNs, H-GMNs are markedly more sample-efficient, and their cost remains nearly constant as metanetwork depth grows, allowing deeper and more expressive processing where flat GMNs run out of memory, without compromising accuracy, which in certain cases considerably improves.

  • Authors: Mojtaba Soltanalian
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CC, math.OC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34734

  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Additional observations can make exact optimization tractable without changing the objective or feasible set. We establish this phenomenon for noisy parity fitting, a worst-case NP-hard problem, with uniformly sampled rank-one sign tensor entries, independent fair factor signs and independent errors. For fixed interaction order $k\ge3$, $n$ signs per block, logarithmic mean degree and known channel biases, $O(n/(\log n)^{1/(k-2)})$ further readings of the same source suffice with high probability for exact optimization in $O(n\log^2 n)$ worst-case work. The leading coefficient is sharp for weak recovery by depth-$O(\log\log n)$ local estimators with independent reading marks. A common signed-field law determines the gain over the generating source, the number of tied optima and the dimension needed for later reweightings. The geometry of the remaining coordinates determines the sharp radius of local verification for high-scoring assignments with high probability.

Experimental results on Cityscapes, ADE20K, Pascal VOC, and COCO-Stuff-164K demonstrate that CIRKDV2 outperforms the state-of-the-art semantic segmentation distillation methods by large margins across various network architectures. Our code and pre-trained models are publicly available at this https URL.

Title: Guidance as Variance Matching

  • Authors: Ali Backour, Artem Lukoianov, Frank Permenter, Tommi Jaakkola, Justin Solomon, Vincent Sitzmann
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34801
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34801
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34801]] Guidance as Variance Matching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34801)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Guidance repels the prediction of a diffusion model away from a weaker reference prediction. It substantially improves sample quality, but its mechanism remains poorly understood. We first find that, at the scales used in practice, guidance does not improve the prediction of the posterior mean (the model's training target); it makes the prediction worse, while samples improve. Second, posterior-mean predictions have less variance than the data, and we find that guidance restores it. We introduce variance-matched guidance: a closed-form rule that picks the guidance scale by matching the variance of the guided predictions to that of the data. Without tuning, it recovers the hand-picked scales of eleven widely used models across image, video, audio, motion, and world generation; to our knowledge, no previous explanation of guidance predicts them. Applied per noise level, it yields a schedule without tuned hyperparameters that users prefer to hand-crafted ones on SD 3.5 Medium. Third, we offer an interpretation of the mechanism behind guidance: each model makes a random error in predicting the posterior mean, and guidance rescales the difference of these errors into a sample with the posterior's covariance, which improves sampling. This view also suggests two new hypotheses that our experiments support: guidance can work with negative scales, and guiding a model with its own checkpoint needs a larger scale than guiding it with an independently trained model.

Title: WorldExpand: Building Coherent 3D Worlds via Sparse Joint Panoramic Expansion

  • Authors: Yuyang Yin, Chenjie Cao, Xuhui Zuo, Yuning Gong, Pengfei Wang, Junta Wu, Zhenwei Wang, Yao Zhao, Tengfei Wang, Yunchao Wei, Chunchao Guo

  • Abstract: Constructing complete 3D worlds from a single panorama is challenging because large portions of the scene remain unobserved. We present WorldExpand, which formulates single-panorama world expansion as the generation of sparse, complementary, and high-resolution panoramic observations. Title: Beyond Feature Fusion: Sensor-Grounded Semantic-Spiking Learning for Robust Object Detection

  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34828

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Underexposure weakens object details, and overexposure obscures boundaries. Spike cameras encode light into high-frequency binary streams. Long-window spike counts give stable appearance cues, while short observations reveal changes in firing rates from sparse measurements. We propose SPIRE, a sensor-grounded semantic-spiking framework for illumination-robust object detection from a single spike stream. SPIRE builds an appearance view from the full packet through detection-oriented illumination adaptation and constructs a sequence of cumulative views from progressively longer prefixes. Title: Repairing the Trustworthiness of Distilled Language Models

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Knowledge distillation enables the deployment of large language models through smaller student models, but the compression process can introduce regressions in trustworthiness even when downstream performance is preserved. Repairing such failures is challenging because the original distillation corpus is often unavailable, while direct preference optimization may alter capabilities inherited from the teacher. We propose a targeted repair framework based on the proximal Bregman response function (PBRF) that addresses both limitations. We estimate the curvature required for PBRF using a surrogate proxy dataset, removing dependence on the original transfer corpus, and extend the framework to preference-based supervision through an odds-ratio objective. Title: Proverbs As A Window On Collective Prescriptions

  • Authors: Tomás Lejarraga, Dirk U. Wulff, Ralph Hertwig
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34832
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34832
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34832]] Proverbs As A Window On Collective Prescriptions(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34832)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Proverbs, as repositories of traditional wisdom, have informed human reasoning for millennia. Yet the collective prescriptions they encode have remained largely unquantified. We introduce a computational framework to analyze nearly 50,000 English-language proverbs. Using large language models, validated against human judgments, we map the topical landscape of proverbs, assess their prescriptive power, and quantify their recommendations on key dimensions of human judgment and behavior. This analysis provides a quantitative map of the collective prescriptions in a culture's proverbial wisdom, revealing a core of prudent, honest, and forward-looking guidance alongside varied advice where circumstances dictate the best course of action.

Title: Full Structure Recovery of Linearized Reed-Solomon Codes

  • Authors: Valentina Astore, Martino Borello, Alain Couvreur
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.IT, math.CO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34835
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34835
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34835]] Full Structure Recovery of Linearized Reed-Solomon Codes(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34835)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Linearized Reed-Solomon codes (LRS) are optimal sum-rank-metric codes that generalize both Reed-Solomon and Gabidulin codes, and benefit from excellent decoding capabilities, making them potentially interesting candidates for post-quantum code-based cryptography. Previous works have investigated the underlying geometric structure of LRS codes, identifying geometric invariants that distinguish them from random codes. Other works have proposed approaches to recovering the LRS structure from an arbitrary generator matrix, together with additional data. However, both the distinguishers and the recovery techniques proposed so far are either not fully general, or have a prohibitive computational complexity. In this paper, we propose a polynomial-time procedure for the full structure recovery of LRS codes from the sole knowledge of an arbitrary generator matrix. Our results show that, like their Hamming and rank metric counterparts, LRS codes cannot be used as they are for McEliece-like encryption. Our approach combines Overbeck-type attacks with suitable matrix manipulations and shortening techniques to recover the evaluation parameters across different ranges of code dimensions.

Title: Diffusion Steering via Adaptive Reward Estimation and Resampling

  • Authors: Abinitha Gourabathina, Sana Tonekaboni, Marzyeh Ghassemi, Akbar Rafiey
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34839

  • Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion models can generate images that satisfy complex prompts, yet often produce images that omit requested objects, attributes, or relationships. We propose TrailMix, a training-free method that uses the base model's distribution of possible outcomes to adaptively guide generation toward better text alignment. Our results show how a model's own possible outcomes can inform reliable and controllable generation.

  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to time series reasoning. To expose this ambiguity, we reformulate time series reasoning from a world-to-series projection perspective, viewing timestamps as explicit indices that connect numerical observations and external events to shared world states.   • Keywords: transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Autoregressive transformers trained on tokenized tabular event streams are a simple and strong baseline for predicting financial events such as fraud. The standard bridge is post-hoc Bayes-optimal thresholding of calibrated probabilities under a cost matrix. We ask whether fine-tuning the model to the decision objective can do better. A KL anchor to the pretrained model and an auxiliary language-modeling term preserve calibration and the generative event model. A sampled, group-normalized (GRPO-style) variant provides the extension point to trajectory-level rewards.

  • Abstract: Federated learning (FL) assumes that a client can perform the computation required to produce its own contribution. We systematize this setting as delegated federated learning (Delegated FL), where data ownership and execution of owner-attributed learning are separated. We develop a framework that examines three dimensions over the same delegated transition: delegate-view privacy, delegated attribution, and constrained-owner feasibility. Our audit of 29 systems reveals a recurring composition gap. Privacy mechanisms protect selected artifacts without necessarily characterizing the accumulated view. Furthermore, computational offloading does not establish whether the remaining workload fits the owner's resource budget. Two controlled studies on Raspberry Pi devices illustrate these gaps. We show that encrypted inputs can coexist with information leakage through an exposed residual and that locally retained labels remain inferable from split-learning gradients. We also show that message authentication can reject tampering without establishing that the executor performed the prescribed computation. Our findings demonstrate why individually established guarantees do not necessarily support the same end-to-end claim and motivate evaluating privacy, attribution, and residual owner cost over the same delegated transition.

Title: Stranger Things: When Objects Appear Without Their Typical Neighbors

  • Authors: Siddhartha Gairola, Jiahao Xie, Anna Kukleva, Francesco Locatello, Bernt Schiele
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34881
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34881
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.34881]] Stranger Things: When Objects Appear Without Their Typical Neighbors(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34881)

  • Abstract: Visual context is a powerful cue for object recognition: scenes inform what, where, and at what scale objects are likely to appear. Yet over-reliance on context becomes a failure mode when models predict objects based on their typical neighbors rather than the objects' own visual features, which we call typicality bias. While extensively studied in the pre-Transformer era, it has often been assumed that architectural and scale improvements would naturally solve it, and a systematic diagnostic across the modern vision stack remains absent. We provide such a diagnostic framework. We measure typicality bias via normalized pointwise mutual information (NPMI) over object pairs, and introduce STRANGE-Bench (Synthetic Typicality-RANked Grounded Evaluation Benchmark): a photorealistic, layout-controlled benchmark spanning 17 sub-tiers from typical to never-co-occurring object pairs. To mitigate it, we turn to data augmentation: existing augmentations are designed for visual diversity, not for breaking co-occurrence shortcuts, and sample from the very distribution that produces typicality bias. We propose Anchor-Atypicality Sampling (AAS), a simple sampling rule for paste-based augmentations that uses the NPMI signal to tilt the training-time co-occurrence distribution toward atypical partners. AAS reduces the typicality gap by up to 1.6 p.p. on natural-image COCO splits and 23.0 p.p. on synthetic STRANGE-Bench across three model families, without sacrificing overall performance.
